particles can be expressed with
n
direct products

\big|Y_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\dots,\ell_{n}}^{m_{1},m_{2},\dots,m_{n}}\big\rangle=\big|Y_{\ell_{1}}^{m_{1}}\big\rangle\otimes\big|Y_{\ell_{2}}^{m_{2}}\big\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes\big|Y_{\ell_{n}}^{m_{n}}\big\rangle

(73)

which form the basis of this direct product space.

The operators for a multi-particle system are direct products, where the operator of the
k^{\text{th}}
subsystem occurs at the
k^{\text{th}}
position in the direct product chain e.g.

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{x}^{(k)}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\hat{S}_{x}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{y}^{(k)}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\hat{S}_{y}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}

(74)

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{z}^{(k)}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\hat{S}_{z}\!\otimes\!\dotsm\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}

where
\hat{S}_{\{x,y,z\}}^{(k)}
is the operator representing an
x-
,
y-
, or
z-
projection of spin on the
k^{\text{th}}
particle in the system of particles.

The Hamiltonians of two non-interacting uncorrelated subsystems must not allow the subsystems to affect each other, giving a composite Hamiltonian of two subsystems as

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1,2}=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\!\otimes\!\bm{\mathbbm{1}}_{2}+\bm{\mathbbm{1}}_{1}\!\otimes\!\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{2}

(75)

where
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
is the identity operator.

In extending this description of spherical tensor operators to coupled particles,
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
can be used to couple two tensors of a given rank to another rank
[
7
]
. The
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
can be interpreted as a unitary transformation from a linear combination of products of single particle operators to two particle operators
[
73
]
. Two particle tensor operators,
\hat{T}_{\ell m}^{(12)}
, from the single particle operators
\hat{T}_{\ell_{1}m_{1}}^{(1)}
and
\hat{T}_{\ell_{2}m_{2}}^{(2)}
defined in this way are

\hat{T}_{\ell m}^{(12)}=\sum\limits_{m_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{m_{1}m_{2}m}^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell}\hat{T}_{\ell_{1}m_{1}}^{(1)}\hat{T}_{\ell_{2}m-m_{1}}^{(2)}

(76)

where
\mathcal{C}_{m_{1}m_{2}m}^{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\ell}
are
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients
. The two particle spherical tensor operators of rank-2 are

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{2\pm 2}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{L}_{\pm}^{2}

(77)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{2\pm 1}^{(2)}=\mp\frac{1}{2}\big(\hat{L}_{z}\hat{L}_{\pm}+\hat{L}_{\pm}\hat{L}_{z}\big)

(78)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{20}^{(2)}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\big(\hat{L}_{z}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\big(\hat{L}_{+}\hat{L}_{-}+\hat{L}_{-}\hat{L}_{+}\big)\big)

(79)

The full list of irreducible spherical tensor operators is given in
appendix

A

table

3
.

§ 4  The Spin Hamiltonian

Spin Hamiltonians are subject to three types of interactions, and can only have three generic algebraic forms
[
73
]
:

Linear in the spin operators

\longrightarrow
Interactions with the static magnetic field,
\mathrm{B_{0}}
, and the irradiated radio/microwave frequency
\mathrm{B_{1}}(t)
field.

Bilinear in the spin operators

\longrightarrow
Interactions between two spins.

Quadratic in the spin operators

\longrightarrow
Indirect interactions e.g. with field gradient, interpreted as the interaction of a spin and itself.

In generating the total Hamiltonian of a arbitrary spin system, it is useful to partition the system into general parts corresponding to different physical processes

\hat{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{Z}}+\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{N\!N}}+\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{N\!E}}+\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{EE}}+\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k}

(80)

Each of the split Hamiltonian parts represents the following interactions

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{Z}}=
Zeeman interactions

\longrightarrow
Chemical shielding tensors and g-tensors.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{N\!N}}=
Inter-Nuclear interactions

\longrightarrow
J-couplings, quadrupolar interactions, dipolar interactions.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\!\mathcal{N\!E}}=
Electron-Nuclear interactions

\longrightarrow
Isotropic and anisotropic hyperfine couplings.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{EE}}=
Inter-Electron interactions

\longrightarrow
Exchange interaction, zero field splitting, dipolar interactions.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k}=
Microwave/Radiofrequency irradiation

\longrightarrow
Irradiated pulses with amplitude and phase, or control pulses with associated amplitude coefficients.

In describing these interactions it is useful to introduce the notation for a vector of spin operators,
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}
, which contains the three Cartesian operators as elements, ordered
\big(\hat{S}_{x},\hat{S}_{y},\hat{S}_{z}\big)
.

§ 1  Zeeman Interactions

An applied external magnetic field,
\vec{B}
induces an energy difference
\Delta E
between the quantised magnetic moment values, with the energy is given by

E=-\vec{\mu}\cdot\vec{B}

(81)

where
\vec{B}
is the external magnetic field. For nucleons, this is the nuclear Zeeman interaction, and for electrons, this is called the electron Zeeman interaction. Its Hamiltonian is

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}\!\cdot\!\bm{A}\!\cdot\!\vec{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{S}_{x}&\hat{S}_{y}&\hat{S}_{z}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{xx}&a_{xy}&a_{xz}\\
a_{yx}&a_{yy}&a_{yz}\\
a_{zx}&a_{zy}&a_{zz}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{x}\\
B_{y}\\
B_{z}\end{pmatrix}

(82)

where the tensor
\bm{A}
is the Zeeman interaction tensor. This is the coupling of the spin dipole moment to the external magnetic field. Without any further interaction, the Zeeman tensor reduces to the constant
\frac{g\mu}{\hbar}
, where
g
is the
g-
factor and
\mu
is the nuclear magneton for nuclei and the Bohr magneton for electrons.

Chemical shielding tensor

The magnetic field also perturbs the electronic structure of the nucleus, with the induced field
\bm{\delta}\vec{B}_{0}
. Using perturbation theory
[
209
]
gives the spin interaction from the contribution of
\bm{\delta}\vec{B}_{0}
as

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{CS}}=\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}\!\cdot\!\bm{\delta}\!\cdot\!\vec{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{S}_{x}&\hat{S}_{y}&\hat{S}_{z}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{xx}&\delta_{xy}&\delta_{xz}\\
\delta_{yx}&\delta_{yy}&\delta_{yz}\\
\delta_{zx}&\delta_{zy}&\delta_{zz}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{x}\\
B_{y}\\
B_{z}\end{pmatrix}

(83)

where
\bm{\delta}
is known as the chemical shielding tensor.

Electron g-tensor

A similar interaction to the perturbation from chemical shielding can be derived for electrons. The energy operator for the electron dipole in an external magnetic field is

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mu_{B}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}\!\cdot\!\bm{g}\!\cdot\!\vec{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{L}_{x}&\hat{L}_{y}&\hat{L}_{z}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}g_{xx}&g_{xy}&g_{xz}\\
g_{yx}&g_{yy}&g_{yz}\\
g_{zx}&g_{zy}&g_{zz}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{x}\\
B_{y}\\
B_{z}\end{pmatrix}

(84)

where
\bm{g}
is known as the
g-
tensor and the constant
\mu_{B}
is the Bohr magneton.

§ 2  Inter-Nuclear Interactions

Inter-nuclear dipolar interactions

Treating nuclear spins as point magnetic dipoles,
\vec{\mu}
, the energy of the interaction between two nuclear dipoles,
\vec{\mu}_{1}
and
\vec{\mu}_{2}
is

E=-\vec{\mu}\!\cdot\!\vec{B}=-\frac{\mu_{0}}{4\pi}\frac{3\big(\vec{\mu}_{1}\!\cdot\!\vec{r}\big)\big(\vec{r}\!\cdot\!\vec{\mu}_{2}\big)-r^{3}\big(\vec{\mu}_{1}\!\cdot\!\vec{\mu}_{2}\big)}{r^{5}}

(85)

where
\vec{r}
is the vector that connects the dipoles, and the constant
\mu_{0}
is the permeability of free space. Hamiltonian is for this interaction between two spins,
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(1)}
and
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(2)}

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{DD}}=-\frac{\mu_{0}}{4\pi}\frac{\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\hbar}{r^{5}}\Big(3\big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(1)}\!\cdot\!\vec{r}\big)\big(\vec{r}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(2)}\big)-r^{2}\big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(1)}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}^{(2)}\big)\Big)

(86)

where
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are the magnetogyric ratios of the two spins.

Spin-rotation coupling

Collections of particles have angular momentum, just as single particles do. A magnetic induction is generated by a collection charges moving at velocity. Using
eq.

82
and considering the motion of the
n^{\text{th}}
charge with mass
m_{n}
and angular momentum operator
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}_{n}
– the spin rotation coupling interaction is

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{SR}}=\sum\limits_{k}\gamma_{k}\vec{B}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}_{k}=-\frac{\mu_{0}}{4\pi}\sum\limits_{n,k}\frac{\gamma_{k}q_{n}}{m_{n}r_{n}^{3}}\Big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}_{n}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}_{k}\Big)

(87)

where
\gamma_{k}
is the magnetogyric ratio of the
k^{\text{th}}
nucleus and
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}_{k}
are their spin operators. Evaluating the sum over the
n
moving charges, the spin-rotation Hamiltonian is

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{SR}}=-\sum\limits_{k}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}\!\cdot\!\bm{A}_{k}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}_{k}

(88)

where
\bm{A}_{k}
are spin-rotation coupling tensors.

Quadrupolar coupling

Nuclei with spin
>\mathrm{spin\!-\!{}^{1}\!/_{\!2}}
have non-spherical charge distribution, and the direction of this
nuclear quadrupole moment
is fixed, it interacts with the spin itself.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{Q}}=\frac{-eQ}{2s(2s-1)}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}\!\cdot\!\bm{V}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}=\frac{-eQ}{2s(2s-1)}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{L}_{x}&\hat{L}_{y}&\hat{L}_{z}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{xx}&v_{xy}&v_{xz}\\
v_{yx}&v_{yy}&v_{yz}\\
v_{zx}&v_{zy}&v_{zz}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{L}_{x}\\
\hat{L}_{y}\\
\hat{L}_{z}\end{pmatrix}

(89)

where
eQ
is the quadrupole moment of the nucleus,
s
is the spin of the nucleus,
-\frac{eQ}{2s(2s-1)}\bm{V}
is the quadrupolar coupling tensor. Quadrupolar coupling only exists for
\mathrm{spin-{1}}
nuclei and higher.

J-coupling

J-coupling is the scalar spin-spin coupling between two spins, after ignoring anisotropy, its Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{J}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{L}_{x}&\hat{L}_{y}&\hat{L}_{z}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&a&0\\
0&0&a\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{S}_{x}\\
\hat{S}_{y}\\
\hat{S}_{z}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=a\big(\hat{L}_{x}\hat{S}_{x}+\hat{L}_{y}\hat{S}_{y}+\hat{L}_{z}\hat{S}_{z}\big)=a\big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}\!\cdot\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{S}}}}\big)

(90)

where
a
is the scalar coupling strength.

§ 3  Microwave & Radiofrequency terms

These interactions are the most important to the area of optimal control – they are the part of the Hamiltonian that can be directly controlled – all else is considered uncontrollable and is summed to a single
drift Hamiltonian
. Commonly referred to as the time dependent
\mathrm{B_{1}}(t)
field, areas of magnetic resonance manipulating nuclear spins use radiofrequency fields, and areas of magnetic resonance manipulating electrons use microwave fields.

Optimal control, set out in
chapter

3
, uses commonly control operators
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{x}
and
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{y}
with their amplitude coefficients becoming the variables of an optimisation algorithm. These amplitude coefficients relate directly to the pulse power in Hz, obtained from pulse calibration on the instrument.

This interaction has the same form as the Zeeman interaction in
eq.

82

\hat{\mathcal{H}}=-\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.06805pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.39096pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.84303pt}}\smash{\vec{L}}}}_{k}\!\cdot\!\gamma_{k}\!\cdot\!\vec{B}_{rf}(t)

(91)

It is normal to polarise the radiofrequency field, of frequency
\omega
, with a phase
\varphi

[
73
]
:

\vec{B}_{rf}=2\mathrm{B_{1}}\cos\Big(\omega t\big(\vec{\e}_{x}\cos\varphi+\vec{\e}_{y}\sin\varphi\big)\Big)

(92)

where
\vec{\e}_{x}
and
\vec{\e}_{y}
are the orthogonal unit vectors in the
x
and
y
directions. The above equation can be decomposed into two counter-rotating components (one of which can be neglected with a high
\mathrm{B_{0}}
field), giving the Hamiltonian as
[
73
]
9
9

9

The factor in the sum of
\frac{1}{2}K
will become apparent when discussing optimal control in detail, but it is common to sum to the total number of control operators, usually
2\times
the number of spins – it is kept here for consistency.

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k}=-\mathrm{B_{1}}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\frac{1}{2}K}\gamma_{k}\Big(\hat{L}_{x}^{(k)}\cos\big(\omega t+\varphi\big)+\hat{L}_{y}^{(k)}\sin\big(\omega t+\varphi\big)\Big)

(93)

and in the time-independent rotating frame, applying a rotation transform, gives:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{(k)}=-\mathrm{B_{1}}\gamma_{k}\hat{L}_{x}^{(k)}\cos\varphi

(94)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{y}^{(k)}=-\mathrm{B_{1}}\gamma_{k}\hat{L}_{y}^{(k)}\sin\varphi

(95)

Optimal Control Theory

“You know what charm is: a way of getting the answer yes without having asked any clear question.”

– Albert Camus,
The Fall

The focus of this thesis is advancing optimal control methods, specifically the
grape
method
[
154
]
. Before this thesis progresses to present a novel optimal control method in
chapter

4
and advances in efficiency of calculation within this method in
chapter

5
, the current chapter will present the theory of optimal control in the context of the
grape
method.

The state of a magnetic resonance system can be controlled using a set of electromagnetic pulses, discrete or continuous in time. Design of these pulses may prove difficult for control of complicated systems; numerical optimisation methods can be used to find a maximum overlap between the desired state and the state produced by the set of pulses, with the pulse schedule being the parameter of the objective function in the optimisation problem.

The art of running an experiment to a given accuracy with minimal expenditure of time and resources is known as
optimal control theory
. A simple interpretation of optimal control theory is the application of numerical optimisation
[
96
,
79
,
17
,
23
,
229
]
to control theory
[
246
,
298
,
92
,
45
,
191
]
. This chapter will be divided into two sections, firstly outlining gradient based numerical optimisation with Newton-type methods, and secondly building a derivation of the optimal control method
grape
.

§ 1  Numerical optimisation

Continuing from
chapter

1
and the example of Newton’s root finding method adapted to find the roots of an analytical gradient in
fig.

1
, this section will formally extend that idea to build a family of generalised optimisation algorithms called Newton-type methods. An optimisation problem can be written as

\displaystyle\min_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}}\Big\{\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\Big\},

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(1)

finding a local minimum, and a transformation of the above problem can find a local maximum:

\displaystyle-\min_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}}\Big\{-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\Big\}=\max_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}}\Big\{\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\Big\},

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(2)

The function
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
is a scalar function of
n
variables called the
objective function
. The maximisation problem of
eq.

2
was recast in the form of a minimisation problem with a simple transform, and it seems sensible to consider only minimisation problems for the remainder of this section.

In this work an objective function should be a smooth function – one which is continuously differentiate – that can be represented by a convergent power series of terms calculated from the derivatives in the vicinity of a point i.e. a local approximation. For a function of one variable, this is

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\bm{h})=\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})+\dfrac{\bm{h}}{1!}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})+\frac{\bm{h}^{2}}{2!}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})+\dots=\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bm{h}^{r}}{r!}\nabla^{r}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})

(3)

where
\bm{h}
is a small displacement to the vector
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
, and
\nabla^{r}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
is the
r^{\text{th}}
order derivative of the function
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
. This series is known as the many variable
Taylor series

[
302
,
201
]
.

The
gradient vector
,
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
, and the
Hessian matrix
,
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
, are introduced here. These two definitions will be used extensively throughout the remainder of this thesis and further explanation of their notation is required:
\nabla
is the gradient operator defined by

\displaystyle\nabla\triangleq\big|\nabla\big\rangle\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(4)

which can be used to construct a Hessian operator
\nabla^{2}
:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\triangleq\big|\nabla\big\rangle\!\big\langle\nabla\big|=\!\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{1}}&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial c_{n}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{1}^{2}}&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{1}\partial c_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{1}\partial c_{n}}\\
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{2}\partial c_{1}}&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{2}^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{2}\partial c_{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{n}\partial c_{1}}&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{n}\partial c_{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial c_{n}^{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\!

(5)

A point in a vector space is denoted by a bold symbol, representing the coordinates of that point e.g.
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}
, where the superscript in parentheses denotes the specific iteration,
\jmath
, of the optimisation. A direction is a line in the vector space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted with Dirac bra-ket symbols e.g.
\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle
. A point that minimises or maximises a function will be denoted with an asterisk,
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}
, and is called a
minimiser
or
maximiser
respectively
1
1

1

It is important to point out that it is only practicable to find a
local minimiser
,
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}
, which may not be a
global minimiser
.
.

In describing optimisation problems, it will be useful to introduce two descriptions of the objective function as a specified point: the
slope
and
curvature
, which are defined at a point,
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}
, as

\displaystyle\textit{slope}\triangleq\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\textit{curvature}\triangleq\big\langle\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle

Figure 1:
Types of stationary points, where the gradient is equal to zeros.
a
shows the minimiser of the function
x^{2}+y^{2}
,
b
shows the maximiser of the function
-x^{2}-y^{2}
, and
c
shows the saddle point of the function
x^{2}-y^{2}
. Each plot has a single point where the gradient is equal to zero.

At the minimiser of a smooth function, two observations hold
2
2

2

for example, see
fig.

1

d
:

1

at the minimiser, the slope is zero

2

at the minimiser, the curvature is non-negative in all directions

The two observations of a point being a minimiser lead to the
necessary conditions
for a local solution to a minimisation problem
[
79
]
. The first, that the slope is zero at a minimiser is equivalent to

\displaystyle\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\implies\lim_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\to\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}}\Big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\Big\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=0

(7)

and is called the
first-order necessary condition
for a minimiser, and can be interpreted as requiring the gradient to be zero at a local minimiser.
fig.

1
illustrates the three different types of
stationary points
satisfy the
first-order necessary condition
. Notice, only
fig.

1

a
shows a minimiser, and
fig.

1

c
has a point of zero gradient that is not a minimiser or a maximiser.
fig.

1
shows by example that
eq.

7
alone is not enough to identify a local minimiser.

The observation concerning curvature of the objective function at the minimiser is required to identify a minimum. This observation is equivalent to

\displaystyle\lim_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\to\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\geqslant 0,

\displaystyle\forall\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}

(8)

and is called the
second-order necessary condition
for a minimiser, and can be interpreted as requiring a positive semi-definite
3
3

3

A positive semi-definite matrix has all eigenvalues
\lambda\geqslant 0
Hessian matrix in the vicinity of a local minimiser. However, a semi-definite matrix will become a problem when an inverse of the Hessian is required. Numerically, it is useful to reformulate this last condition as

\displaystyle\lim_{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\to\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle>0,

\displaystyle\forall\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\neq\mathbf{0}

(9)

which requires the Hessian matrix to be positive definite
4
4

4

A positive semi-definite matrix has all eigenvalues
\lambda>0
in the vicinity of a local minimiser.
eq.

7
together with
eq.

9
set out the
sufficient conditions
for a local minimiser, and can be checked numerically. Returning again to
fig.

1
, it is clear that
fig.

1

a
has positive curvature in all direction around the vicinity of the minimiser,
fig.

1

b
has negative curvature around the maximiser, and
fig.

1

c
as a mixture of positive and negative curvature around the saddle point. These sufficient conditions for an optimal solution will be used extensively in
chapter

4
, and will be elaborated there.

§ 1  The gradient ascent method

In searching for an optimum, the desired minimum or maximum of an objective function, a general strategy must be developed to find new points giving better objective function values. Two popular strategies are the
restricted step methods
5
5

5

also known as the
trust region methods
.
and the
line search method
. This chapter will be concerned with the
line search method
, and
chapter

4
will be concerned with
restricted step methods
.

An example of a strategy to perform a line search at an iteration
\jmath
is

1

find a search direction
\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}

2

find a search length,
\varepsilon
, to minimise or maximise
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})
with respect to
\varepsilon
.

3

set the new iteration,
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}=\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}
.

Step
2
is an idealised solution called an
exact line search
, and is conceptually useful but not practicable in a finite number of operations. The subproblem of finding
\varepsilon
will be outlined in
section

4
, for now it is sufficient to use the concept of an exact line search.

Line search methods introduce the search direction
\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}
, at an iteration
\jmath
. One choice of search direction is that of the gradient. For minimisation, the search direction should be antiparallel to the gradient, and for maximisation the search direction should be parallel to the gradient:

\displaystyle\textit{minimisation},

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}

\displaystyle=-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

(10a)

\displaystyle\textit{maximisation},

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}

\displaystyle=\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

(10b)

Together with an adequate line search method, these two methods are called the
gradient descent method
for minimisation and the
gradient ascent method
for maximisation. The update to the objective variable using a scaled step length found from a line search is

\displaystyle\textit{minimisation}:

\displaystyle\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}=\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}-\varepsilon\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

(11a)

\displaystyle\textit{maximisation}:

\displaystyle\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}=\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

(11b)

These methods can also be used when no derivative information is available, although less effective, by using an estimate of the derivatives:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})=\frac{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}(1+h))-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})}{h}+\mathcal{O}(h)

(12a)

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})=\frac{\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}(1+h))-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}(1-h))}{2h}+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})

(12b)

where
h=\sqrt{\textsc{eps}}
is a small perturbation to
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
. These two approximations of the gradient are called finite difference approximations,
eq.

12a
is the forward differences approximation, and
eq.

12b
is the central differences approximation. In a similar way, the second derivatives can be approximated with finite differences of the first derivatives. In addition to their use when no derivative information is available, these approximations are also useful in checking derivative calculations when derivative information is available.

§ 2  Newton’s Method

Considering that the linear approximation of a function, such as gradient descent/ascent methods, will fail to differentiate between an extremum and a stationary point, as in
fig.

1
, more is required in the form of the sufficient conditions already set out in
eq.

9
. A useful model in numerical optimisation is the
quadratic model
of the function to be optimised. Although the optimisation methods should work on many functions, not just quadratic functions, in deriving the methods for optimisation the
quadratic model
has proved fruitful. Desirable properties of this model are
[
79
]
:

1

Quadratic functions are smooth, with a well defined minimum.

2

A continuously differentiable function is locally well approximated by a quadratic function near a minimum.

3

More accurate than a linear approximation of a function, as in gradient descent/ascent methods, and higher derivative orders will likely be small.

4

Can be made invariant under linear transformation of variables
6
6

6

An inductive proof of this property is given for Newton-like methods is given in
[
79
]
.
.

The aim of using a
quadratic model
of a function is to find its extremum in a single iteration by analytically solving the model of the function to find its single stationary point shown in
fig.

1
. Given an initial starting point of
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0}
, a quadratic model of the objective function at an extremum is a truncated Taylor series:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\triangleq\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0}+\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta})\approx\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})+\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big\rangle

(13)

This equation is solved to find the vector
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}{}
required to step from the initial point
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0}
to the extremum
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}
. The solution is found when the derivative of
eq.

13
, with respect to the step
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}{}
, is equal to zero
7
7

7

eq.

14
requires the Hessian matrix to be symmetric,
\nabla^{2}=(\nabla^{2})^{\dagger}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\dd}{\dd\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0}+\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta})=

\displaystyle\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big|+\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})=\big\langle\mathbf{0}\big|

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big\rangle+\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big\rangle=\big|\mathbf{0}\big\rangle

(14)

This gives two solutions: either
\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big\rangle=\big|\mathbf{0}\big\rangle
which means the starting point is an extremum,
\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0}=\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast}
; or the non-trivial solution

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{\delta}\big\rangle=-\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})\big\rangle

(15)

Specifically, this model is true for the function in
fig.

1

a
, which has one minimum and the function must be minimised. To be able to find the maximum in
fig.

1

b
, a small modification must be made to
eq.

13
. There are now two conditions, one is a minimisation condition that
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\leqslant\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})
and the other is the maximisation condition that
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})\geqslant\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})
:

\displaystyle\textit{minimisation}:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})

\displaystyle\leqslant 0

(16a)

\displaystyle\textit{maximisation}:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\ast})-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{0})

\displaystyle\geqslant 0

(16b)

Using the Newton step found in
eq.

15
, these conditions lead to the search directions

\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\textsc{Newton}}\big\rangle=-\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big\rangle

(17)

where the direction of ascent/descent is accounted for by a positive/negative definite Hessian matrix. If a
linear model
of the objective function is used, the Taylor series truncates after the gradient term in
eq.

13
and the update formulae would be described by
eqs.

10a
,
10b
, the gradient descent/ascent methods using an exact line search. Following the modification of the exact line search, a Newton method using a line search method would modify the update rule to

\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}\big\rangle=\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle-\varepsilon\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle

(18)

where
\varepsilon
is calculated with an appropriate line search method in
section

4
. The final case of using a Newton method to find (or avoid) the stationary point in
fig.

1

c
is an important one, but left to
chapter

4
.

§ 3  Quasi-Newton Methods

Although Newton’s method has attractive quadratic convergence properties, it has the obvious disadvantage of needing a quadratic number of Hessian element calculations. Moreover, the formulae for the Hessian elements must be supplied. The disadvantages associated with a Hessian calculation can be avoided by using a popular class of
quasi-Newton methods
. These methods are similar to Newton’s method, except the Hessian is approximated by a symmetric, positive definite matrix,
\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}
which is updated at every iteration with information from gradient calculations.

\displaystyle\textit{minimisation}:

\displaystyle\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\textsc{qn}}\big\rangle=-\big[\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big\rangle

(19a)

\displaystyle\textit{maximisation}:

\displaystyle\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\textsc{qn}}\big\rangle=+\big[\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big\rangle

(19b)

A procedure to update the Hessian approximation follows

1

set the current search direction
\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle=\pm\big[\mathbf{H}_{\jmath}\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle

2

perform a line search to find a step length,
\varepsilon
, giving
\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}\big\rangle=\big|\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle+\varepsilon\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle

3

update the Hessian approximation,
\mathbf{H}_{\jmath}
, with an appropriate method to give a new approximation
\mathbf{H}_{\jmath+1}

Maximisation and minimisation are accounted for with
\pm
: maximisation with positive direction; minimisation with negative direction, allowing a strictly positive definite Hessian matrix.

A useful property of this procedure is that the Hessian approximation can use any gradient information obtained in the line search. Without better information, it is normal to set the initial Hessian approximation equal to the identity matrix
\mathbf{H}_{0}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
. An advantage of
quasi-Newton methods
over other methods, including the
conjugate gradient method

[
124
,
177
]
, is that the quasi-Newton iteration does not need to be restarted periodically
[
79
]
.

After repeated updating, the matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}
should approximate the Hessian matrix
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
, and one method to achieve this is to use the difference of gradients from iteration to iteration. For brevity, the change in objective variable and the change in gradient will be denoted by

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}

\displaystyle\triangleq\Big(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}-\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\Big)

(20a)

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}

\displaystyle\triangleq\Big(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1})-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\Big)

(20b)

Using a truncated Taylor series of the gradient, with a quadratic model of the objective, an equation for the change in gradient can be derived:

\displaystyle\big|\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle+\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle+\dots

\displaystyle\implies\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle

(21)

Since the Hessian approximation is calculated after a line search as the final stage of an iteration,
\mathbf{H}_{\jmath+1}
correctly relates the gradient change in
eq.

21
, giving

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle=\mathbf{H}_{\jmath+1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle

(22)

which is known as the
secant equation

[
229
]
. It maps the symmetric, positive-definite Hessian to the gradient, which is only possible if

\big[x_{k+1}-x_{k}\big]^{T}\big[\nabla f_{k+1}-\nabla f_{k}\big]>0

(23)

This is known as the
curvature condition
and is satisfied for convex function
[
229
]
.

Alternatively, the matrix approximating the inverse to the Hessian can be updated, reducing the computational load
8
8

8

The inverse of a matrix is regarded as an expensive matrix operation that can be ill-defined
[
106
]
.

\displaystyle\textit{minimisation}:

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\textsc{qn}}=-\big[\mathbf{I}_{\vphantom{|}}\big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big\rangle

(24a)

\displaystyle\textit{maximisation}:

\displaystyle\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\textsc{qn}}=+\big[\mathbf{I}_{\vphantom{|}}\big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big\rangle

(24b)

A similar procedure as above updates the approximation to the inverse-Hessian,
\mathbf{I}_{\jmath}
, except the search direction does not involve an inverse of a matrix:
\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle=\pm\big[\mathbf{H}_{\jmath}\big]^{-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle
. Similarly, the change in gradient is used to approximate the inverse-Hessian with

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle=\big[\mathbf{I}_{\jmath+1}\big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle

(25)

which is known as the
quasi-Newton condition

[
79
]
.

Rank-1 Hessian update

Initial attempts to find a method of updating the Hessian approximation that ensures its symmetry and satisfies
eq.

22
, resulted in a method called the
symmetric rank-1
or
sr1
method
[
30
,
47
]
. A rank-1 symmetric matrix can be constructed from the outer product of a real vector with itself,
\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\mathbf{u}\big|
, giving the symmetric rank-1 update to the Hessian approximation

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}+a\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\mathbf{u}\big|

(26)

where
a
is an arbitrary constant and
\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle
is a vector to be determined. Substituting the last equation into the secant equation,
eq.

22
, gives

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle+a\Big[\big\langle\mathbf{u}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\mathbf{u}\big\rangle

(27)

The vector
\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle
must be proportional to
\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle
, with proportionality absorbed into the arbitrary constant
a
, giving

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle=a\bigg[\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\Big(\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big)\bigg]\Big(\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big)

(28)

This is satisfied for
a
if
[
229
]

a=\frac{\sign{\bigg[\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\Big(\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big)\bigg]}}{\bigg[\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\Big(\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big)\bigg]}

(29)

and gives the only symmetric rank-1 update, satisfying
eq.

22
, to be

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}+\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}-\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle-\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}

(30)

A similar derivation can be made to find a symmetric rank-1 update that satisfies
eq.

25
, giving an update rule for the inverse-Hessian approximation. Alternatively, a useful formula to use
eq.

30
to find a corresponding update rule for the inverse-Hessian approximation is the
Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula

[
106
]
9
9

9

Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula:

[
106
]

\big(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{T}\big)^{-1}=\mathbf{A}^{-1}-\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{U}\big(\bm{\mathbbm{1}}+\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{U}\big)^{-1}\mathbf{V}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}

with
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and both
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
. This shows a rank-
k
update to a matrix gives a rank-
k
update to its inverse.
. Using the formula for a rank-1 update amounts to the interchanges
\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}\longleftrightarrow\mathbf{I}_{\vphantom{|}}
and
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\longleftrightarrow\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
, giving
10
10

10

This is a property of a
self-dual formula
.

\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}+\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}-\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}-\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle-\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}

(31)

eqs.

30
,
31
both ensure any update is a symmetric matrix, however, the update is not guaranteed to be positive definite
[
229
,
79
]
. This drawback may be alleviated by forcing a positive definite matrix with a method described in
chapter

4
. However, there is a second problem with
eqs.

30
,
31
, that the denominator may vanish.

Rank-2 Hessian update

In an attempt to avoid the problems of using
eqs.

30
,
31
, a more flexible Hessian update formula can be constructed be replacing the rank-1 update of
eq.

26
with a rank-2 update

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}+a\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\mathbf{u}\big|+b\big|\mathbf{v}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\mathbf{v}\big|

(32)

Using the secant equation,
eq.

22
, and substituting into this rank-2 update gives

\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle+a\Big[\big\langle\mathbf{u}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\mathbf{u}\big\rangle+b\Big[\big\langle\mathbf{v}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\Big]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\mathbf{v}\big\rangle

(33)

There was only one symmetric rank-1 solution to
eq.

22
, there are a family of symmetric rank-2 update formulae which will be summarised at the end of this section in a single equation of one variable,
eqs.

39a
,
39b
, for a Hessian approximation and an inverse-Hessian approximation.

To start a derivation of the general rank-2 formulae
eqs.

39a
,
39b
a set of non-unique solutions to
eq.

33
are trialled, while ensuring the solutions satisfy the curvature condition of
eq.

23
:
\big|\mathbf{u}\big\rangle=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle
and
\big|\mathbf{v}\big\rangle=\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle
, giving the arbitrary constants
[
79
]

\displaystyle a=\frac{1}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}

\displaystyle b=\frac{-1}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}

(34)

This gives one of two special cases to a general formula to compute the approximation to the Hessian matrix is
[
229
]
, which in itself can be used as a rank-2 update approximating a Hessian:

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}+\Bigg[\bm{\mathbbm{1}}+\frac{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}\Bigg]\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}\\
-\Bigg[\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}+\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}\Bigg]

(35)

The formula is called the
dfp
updating formula
11
11

11

The method is commonly named after the authors Davidon, Fletcher, and Powell. It also known as the
variable metric algorithm
.
and was originally published in a technical paper
[
46
,
48
]
and later presented using
eq.

32
by Fletcher and Powell
[
80
]
. Again, using the
Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula

[
106
]
, the update in
eq.

35
can be transformed to update an inverse-Hessian approximation:

\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}-\Bigg[\frac{\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}\Bigg]+\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}

(36)

In comparison to the
sr1
updates in
eqs.

30
,
31
, the
dfp
updates in
eqs.

35
,
36
ensure a positive definite update, which can be proved with a Cholesky factorisation
12
12

12

if a matrix
\mathbf{A}
is positive definite, there exists a factorisation
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\dagger}
where
\mathbf{L}
is a lower triangular matrix with real, positive diagonal elements. This is called a Cholesky factorisation
[
106
]
of
\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath}
. There still exists a drawback with these update methods, that they need an accurate line search
[
79
]
, which can become computationally expensive when gradient calculations are needed for a line search (see
section

4
).

Although the
sr1
formulae in
eqs.

30
,
31
are
self-dual
, it is obvious that the
dfp
formulae in
eqs.

35
,
36
are not. An important formula, called the
bfgs
formula
[
31
,
32
,
78
,
100
,
280
]
13
13

13

named after the authors Broyden, Fletcher, Goldfarb, and Shanno
, can be constructed by observing
\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
and finding the
dual formula
of
eq.

36
by interchanging
\mathbf{H}_{\vphantom{|}}\longleftrightarrow\mathbf{I}_{\vphantom{|}}
and
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\longleftrightarrow\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
:

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}-\Bigg[\frac{\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle}\Bigg]+\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}

(37)

A
bfgs
update for an approximation to the inverse-Hessian can be found by transforming this last formula with the
Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula

[
106
]
:

\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}+\Bigg[\bm{\mathbbm{1}}+\frac{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}\Bigg]\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}\\
-\Bigg[\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big|\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}+\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}\big\rangle}\Bigg]

(38)

The
bfgs
update formulae are widely accepted to perform better than the
dfp
formulae
[
79
,
96
,
229
]
, requiring a line search with comparatively fewer inner iterations.

The
dfp
and
bfgs
are both special cases for the general
Broyden family
rank-2 formulae
[
30
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{\phi}_{\jmath+1}

\displaystyle=\big(1-\phi\big)\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath+1}+\phi\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}

(39a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{\phi}_{\jmath+1}

\displaystyle=\big(1-\phi\big)\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{dfp}}_{\jmath+1}+\phi\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}

(39b)

with
\phi\in[0,1]
.

In considering the benefit of calculating the inverse-Hessian approximation of
eqs.

38
,
36
, that there is no need to find the inverse of a matrix explicitly, there is a drawback – as will be pointed out in
chapter

4
, the inverse-Hessian approximations do not allow the implementation of
trust region methods
and other conditioning methods.

Limited memory quasi-Newton methods

Large optimisation problems may require Hessian approximations which are not sparse and cannot be computed at a reasonable cost. Rank-1 and rank-2 updates only add further vector information to the to the Hessian approximation, and the end use of the Hessian information is to calculate a vector search direction.
Limited memory quasi-Newton methods

[
228
]
make use of a store of the
m
most recent vectors used to describe the Hessian approximation – saving storage space if
m<n
, where
n
is the number of objective function variables. The following review of this class of methods is from
[
229
]
. As the
\ell
-
bfgs
(limited-memory
bfgs
) method
[
194
,
34
]
is a popular limited-memory quasi-Newton method in optimal control
[
86
]
(and more compactly derived), the following derivation will follow this update to the inverse Hessian approximation. Before proceeding, it will be useful to present
eq.

38
in a product form
[
228
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath+1}=\mathbf{V}_{\jmath}^{\dagger}\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}\mathbf{V}_{\jmath}+\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath}\big\rangle},

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{\jmath}=\Bigg[\bm{\mathbbm{1}}-\frac{\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath}\big|}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath}\big\rangle}\Bigg]

(40)

Considering that quasi-Newton methods are updates to the Hessian, or inverse-Hessian approximation from vectors describing the change in objective function,
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
, and the change in its gradient
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}
– a Hessian approximation at an iteration
\jmath
can be recursively calculated from a store of these vectors and the initial Hessian approximation at
\jmath=\jmath-m
i.e.
m
iterations before the current iteration
\jmath
.

\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}=\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-1}^{\jmath-m}\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}^{\dagger}\Bigg]\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath-m}\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-m}^{\jmath-1}\!\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}\Bigg]\\
\hfill+\frac{1}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m}\big\rangle}\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-1}^{\jmath-m+1}\!\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}^{\dagger}\Bigg]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m}\big|\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-m+1}^{\jmath-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}\Bigg]\\
\hfill+\frac{1}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m+1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m+1}\big\rangle}\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-1}^{\jmath-m+2}\!\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}^{\dagger}\Bigg]\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m+1}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m+1}\big|\Bigg[\prod\limits_{k=\jmath-m+2}^{\jmath-1}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mathbf{V}_{k}\Bigg]\\
\hfill+\cdots+\frac{1}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\big\rangle}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\big\rangle\!\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\big|

(41)

where the ordered products of
\mathbf{V}_{k}
and its transpose
\mathbf{V}_{k}^{\dagger}
form ordered products. This formula lends itself to a recursive algorithm to calculate the inverse-Hessian approximation given a store of objective and gradient vectors. However, the inverse-Hessian approximation
\mathbf{I}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath-m}
must still be stored, defeating the point of the algorithm. The proposition of the limited-memory methods is to use a scaling matrix
\mathbf{I}_{0}
, computed at each update
[
228
]
:

\mathbf{I}^{0}_{\jmath}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\frac{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\big\rangle}{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\big\rangle}

(42)

Further to a recursive algorithm to update the inverse-Hessian approximation, a short-cut can be taken to update the search directional of
eqs.

19a
,
19b
directly. A popular
\ell
-
bfgs
method factors in the gradient to a two-loop recursive algorithm
[
228
,
194
]
to give the search direction
14
14

14

The
\ell
-
bfgs
two-loop recursive algorithm is the implementation in
Spinach
, and the one used in all
\ell
-
bfgs
optimisations of this thesis
. It should be noted that the
\ell
-
bfgs
method is expected to perform badly when applied to ill-conditioned problems, where the Hessian proper has a wide range of eigenvalues
[
229
]
and the scaling matrix fails to be effective. This will be investigated in the context of optimal control in
chapter

4
.

To derive similar recursive update algorithms for other quasi-Newton methods is not so neat because a similar product form of
eq.

40
has not been derived for other Broyden class methods
[
229
]
. However, an “outer product” representation of quasi-Newton methods is useful in deriving other limited-memory algorithms and shows a compact form for general quasi-Newton methods. For the
\ell
-
bfgs
Hessian approximation of
eq.

37
, and using the diagonal scaling matrix
\mathbf{H}_{0}=\big[\mathbf{I}_{0}\big]^{-1}
, this compact notation is
[
34
]
:

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{bfgs}}_{\jmath}=\mathbf{H}_{0}-\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{G}_{\!\jmath}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{0}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\big\rangle&\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}\\
\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}^{\dagger}&-\mathbf{K}_{\jmath}\end{bmatrix}^{\!\!-1}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}^{\dagger}\\
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{G}_{\!\jmath}^{\dagger}\end{bmatrix}

(43)

where the stores of
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}
and
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}
are
n\times m
matrices defined by

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m}&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m+1}&\cdots&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\end{bmatrix}

(44a)

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{G}_{\!\jmath}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m}&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m+1}&\cdots&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\end{bmatrix}

(44b)

\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}
is an
m\times m
matrix defined by

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}\Big)_{\!\!p,q}\triangleq\begin{cases}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m-1+p}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m-1+q}\big\rangle\\
0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}p>q\\
p\leqslant q\end{array}

and
\mathbf{K}_{\jmath}
is a diagonal
m\times m
matrix defined by

\mathbf{K}_{\jmath}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m}\big\rangle&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-m+1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-m+1}\big\rangle&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}_{\jmath-1}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{g}_{\jmath-1}\big\rangle\end{bmatrix}

(47)

The central matrix in
eq.

43
is of small size,
2m\times 2m
, when compared to the number of optimisation variables,
n
: their factorisation requires negligible amount of computation
[
229
]
. This
\ell
-
bfgs
method is an update to the Hessian approximation, and so allows the implementation of Hessian conditioning methods.

Using the definitions above, an
\ell
-
sr1
algorithm can be derived as
[
34
]
:

\mathbf{H}^{\textsc{sr1}}_{\jmath}=\mathbf{H}_{0}+\!\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{G}_{\!\jmath}\!-\mathbf{H}_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\Big]\!\Big[\mathbf{K}_{\jmath}+\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}+\mathbf{L}_{\jmath}^{\dagger}-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}^{\dagger}\mathbf{H}_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\Big]^{\!\!-1}\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\mathbf{G}_{\!\jmath}\ -\mathbf{H}_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\Delta\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{C}_{\!\jmath}\Big]^{\!\dagger}

(48)

Further compact notations can be derived for all the Broyden class of quasi-Newton methods, for inverse-Hessian and Hessian approximations, allowing design of their limited-memory algorithms
[
34
]
.

§ 4  Line search subproblem

This section will present criteria for an acceptable point using the scalar quantity called the
step length
, mentioned in
eqs.

11a
,
11b
,
18
,
21
, deciding how far to move along the search direction.

The
linear approximation
to a function in
section

1
, and the
quadratic approximation
to a function in
sections

2
,
3
, usually need more than their derived search direction to find an acceptable point. Although the gradient based methods will step in the correct direction, without a step length, the methods could wildly misjudge a predicted extremum. Algorithms to find an appropriate step length are called
line search methods
or
inexact line search
. The update to an objective variable at the next iteration is

\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1}=\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}

(49)

where the search direction is calculated with a method chosen from
eqs.

10a
,
10b
,
17
,
19a
,
19b
,
24a
,
24b
. The line search subproblem can be defined as a minimisation
15
15

15

A maximisation problem follows a similar definition

\max_{\varepsilon>0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

The rest of this section will follow a minimisation problem, but a maximisation problem is easily accounted for.
problem in itself:

\min_{\varepsilon>0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})

(50)

The exact minimisation defined by
eq.

50
is not required, and may not find this solution in a finite number of iterations, only an approximation of
\varepsilon
is required to progress the optimisation procedure.

In deciding if the approximation is acceptable, a number of conditions should be tested. The first of these conditions is trivial,
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath+1})<\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})
, and should be accounted for with calculation of a search direction giving a descent direction:

\big\langle\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\rangle<0

(51)

However, this alone does not guarantee convergence. There must be
sufficient decrease
in the slope,
\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}\big\rangle
, of the objective along the search direction. Assuming that an iterative algorithm produces calculated step lengths,
\varepsilon
, the acceptable step length should satisfy

Armijo

condition:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\leqslant\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})+\kappa_{1}\varepsilon\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\in(0,1)

(52)

for a pre-defined constant,
\kappa_{1}
. This condition is known as the
sufficient decrease condition
or the
Armijo condition

[
8
]
, and the constant is chosen to be small, usually
\kappa_{1}\sim 10^{-4}
.

The condition of
eq.

52
will be satisfied for small
\varepsilon
, and the line search algorithm may make insufficient progress by underestimating an acceptable step length. In addition to the condition in
eq.

52
, a condition known as the
Goldstein condition

[
104
]
, ensures that the step length is not underestimated:

Goldstein

condition:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})
