\displaystyle\geqslant\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})+(1-\kappa_{1})\varepsilon\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\in(0,{}^{1}\!/_{\!2})

(53)

where the bounds of
\kappa_{1}
are now more narrow than in
eq.

52
.

An alternative to
eq.

53
in avoiding underestimation of the step length is to account for curvature. This requires the gradient to be calculated at each step length calculation but should give a much better estimation of
eq.

50
. The
curvature condition

[
333
,
334
]
requires
\varepsilon
to satisfy

Curvature

condition:

\displaystyle\big\langle\nabla\!\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle\geqslant\kappa_{2}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle,

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}\in(\kappa_{1},1)

(54)

This ensures that the slope found during step length iteration,
\varepsilon_{j}
, is greater than the slope at
\varepsilon=1
. The constant
\kappa_{2}
is usually chosen to be relatively large for quasi-Newton and Newton methods
[
229
]
,
\kappa_{2}\simeq 0.9
, but should be reduced for gradient descent methods,
\kappa_{2}\simeq 0.1
.
eqs.

52
,
54
are known as the
Wolfe conditions

[
333
,
334
]
.

The
sufficient decrease condition
stipulates that the decrease in
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
must be proportional to the step length and directional derivative. We can extend the
weak curvature condition
of
eq.

54
to ensure that the step length is in the region of a local minimum, ensuring the derivative in
eq.

54
is not too positive, by using a
strong curvature condition

[
333
,
334
]
:

Strong Wolfe

condition:

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle\nabla\!\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}+\varepsilon\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle\Big|\geqslant\kappa_{2}\Big|\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle\Big|,

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}\in(\kappa_{1},1)

(55)

This equation, together with
eq.

52
, are known collectively as the
strong Wolfe conditions
. These conditions are particularly effective when used in a quasi-Newton method from
section

3
.

Backtracking line search

Perhaps the most simple line search method is to iteratively reduce the step length by a predefined factor until
eq.

52
is satisfied. This algorithm is called the
backtracking line search
, and is useful when gradient calculations are accurate but expensive. The iterative reduction of
\varepsilon
follows

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j+1}=\beta\varepsilon_{j}

while

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\varepsilon_{j}\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\geqslant\kappa_{1}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big|\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath}\big\rangle

(56)

where
\beta\in(0,1)
, continuing until
eq.

52
is satisfied, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow\varepsilon_{j}
.The initial step length is chosen as the Newton step,
\varepsilon_{0}=1
, for quasi-Newton and Newton methods. The initial step length can be chosen to be larger for gradient methods and sometimes quasi-Newton methods to avoid small steps at the initial iterations of an optimisation, far from an extremum
16
16

16

A suggestion in
[
229
]
is to set
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{\big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\bm{c}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\jmath})\big\|_{\infty}}
.

Bracketing & sectioning line search

A bracketing and sectioning line search starts with
bracketing phase
which contains an interval of acceptable points,
[a,b]
. Once the bracket is identified, a
sectioning phase
follows dividing the bracket to give a sequence of brackets,
[a_{i},b_{i}]
, where their length tends to zero
[
79
]
.

Assuming gradients are available and they are cheap to calculate
17
17

17

It will be outlined later in this chapter, in
section

5
, that gradients needed in optimal control used in this thesis are particularly cheap to calculate, following at little extra cost to calculating the required objective function value.
, the bracketing and sectioning line search can use the Wolfe conditions in
eqs.

52
,
54
,
55
.

The initial step in the bracketing phase would be choosing an initial step length, usually this is the Newton-step
\varepsilon_{0}=1
as in the backtracking line search. Assuming a lower bound on an acceptable point,
J_{\min}
, and a line search is restricted to the interval
(0,\mu]
where

\mu=\frac{J_{\min}-\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})}{\kappa_{1}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})}

(57)

a general procedure starting from
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{0}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})
at an iteration (the optimisation iteration
\jmath
does not change during a line search)
\jmath
, set out in
[
79
]
is

1

Set
j=1
.

2

Calculate
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{j}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})
, where
0<\varepsilon_{j}\leqslant\mu
.

3

If
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{j}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})\leqslant J_{\min}
,

terminate
with exit message
Boundary
J_{\min}
has been reached
.

4

If
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{j}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})>\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})+\varepsilon_{j}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c})
or
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{j}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})\geqslant\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}J(\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{c}+\varepsilon_{j-1}\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}{}_{\vphantom{|}}\bm{d})
go to

.I
, else go to

.

Then

and

.

§ 5  Comparison of Newton-type methods

Figure 2:
Comparison of gradient based optimisation methods on the Rosenbrock function
[
252
]
, with a backtracking line-search method. The optimisation algorithms terminate when
f(x)<5\times 10^{-2}
.
a
– gradient descent method, taking
376
iterations with
5101
functional evaluations.
b
– symmetric rank-1 update, taking
33
iterations with
185
functional evaluations.
c
–
bfgs
rank-2 update, taking
29
iterations with
78
functional evaluations.
d
– Newton-Raphson method, taking
16
iterations with
43
functional evaluations.

A common test for optimisation methods is on the Rosenbrock function
(1-x)^{2}+100(y-x^{2})^{2}

[
252
]
. The function has one minimum at
(1,1)
, but the difficulty in its minimisation is that the valley is very flat. Although it is easy to find the valley, once there, optimisation usually converges slowly to the point
(1,1)
.

fig.

2
compares the convergence of the gradient descent method of
eq.

11a
, the Newton-Raphson method of
eq.

18
, and two quasi-Newton method of symmetric rank-1 update,
eq.

31
, and the
bfgs
rank-2 update in
eq.

38
.

It is clear that the gradient descent method performs badly, even when using an analytical gradient. The convergence trajectory is seen zig-zagging in small steps on either side of the valley. It is clear that the gradient used in this way is quite ineffective. The Newton-Raphson method performs the best, with an analytic Hessian to use local curvature information in finding the best route down the valley. The quasi-Newton methods also perform quite well, starting from a gradient descent iteration, then being able to approximate the curvature within the valley to find the best route, although taking smaller steps compared to the Newton-Raphson method. Comparing the two quasi-Newton methods, the rank-1 and rank-2 updates both have a similar trajectory iteration count, however, the rank-1 update puts a greater load on the line search algorithm – needing more than twice the amount of gradient evaluations to get similar curvature information compared to the rank-2 update.

§ 2  Gradient AscentPulse Engineering

The task of taking a quantum system from one state to another to a specified accuracy with minimal expenditure of time and energy, with the emphasis on the word
minimal
, is increasingly important in physics and engineering
[
311
,
159
,
154
,
227
]
.

Optimal control can be thought of as an algorithm; there is a start and a stop. in the language of physics; a dynamic system with a initial state,
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
, and a desired destination state,
\big|\sigma\big\rangle
. A solution should be found with a minimum of effort, an optimal amount of effort. In forming a problem for use in a numerical optimisation algorithm, the first step is to create a system model. Within this model the controllable parameters are exposed then fed into an optimisation algorithm.

§ 1  Bilinear systems

The Hamiltonian of a practical
nmr
quantum system can be split into two parts
[
152
]
: that which is beyond the control of the instrument and a
control
part that measurement instrumentation can vary within certain limits.

\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}(t)=\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}+i\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}

(58)

\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}
is the Hamiltonian describing the internal part of the system called the
drift
or
free Hamiltonian
, and
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}
are the
K
parts of the Hamiltonian that can be changed externally, called the
controls
, and
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)
are their time-dependent coefficients. In the case of magnetic resonance the instrumentally controllable subsystem consists of radio frequency/microwave control fields defined in
section

3
.
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}
is the relaxation superoperator characterising dissipation from the system, and has been separated from
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}
for convenience
[
250
,
103
,
166
]
.

Systems with equations of motion that are linear both in the drift and controls
[
2
]
, as in
eq.

58
, are known as
bilinear control systems

[
291
,
274
,
336
,
275
]
in classical control theory. Optimal control of the bilinear system in
eq.

58
is the task of finding the time-dependent control amplitudes
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)
– controlling the system with their corresponding controls
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}
, while under the influence of the time-independent drift
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}
19
19

19

Relaxation will be neglected in many of the derivations in this thesis. For convenience, it will be considered an uncontrollable
damping term
. Although this is only an approximation, the control of open systems is beyond the scope of this thesis.
– taking the system from a defined initial state,
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
, to a desired final state,
\big|\sigma\big\rangle
, a time
T
later.

§ 2  Piecewise constant approximation

Figure 3:
Digram of the piecewise-constant approximation, for 20 equal time slices of length
\Delta t
. Pulses are performed simultaneously in the
x
and
y
directions of the transverse magnetisation plane.

The evolution of a quantum ensemble is governed by the Liouville-von Neumann equation (
eq.

52
). Using a column-wise vector representation of the density operator,
\hat{\rho}
, the Liouville-von Neumann equation has the general solution from
eq.

58
. With the separated Hamiltonian formalism of
eq.

58
, this gives

\big|{\rho}(T)\big\rangle=\exp_{(\text{o})}{\!\!\Bigg[\!-i\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{T}\!\!\Big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}+i\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}\Big)\dd t\Bigg]}\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle

(59)

An approximation of the integral in
eq.

59
can be made by assuming that
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)
are piecewise-constant
[
43
,
154
]
, depicted in
fig.

3
;
slicing
the problem into discrete time intervals to obtain control sequences

\displaystyle{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)\longrightarrow\bm{c}_{k}^{\vphantom{|}}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t_{1})&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t_{2})&\cdots&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t_{N})\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{N}

(60)

where the individual elements
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t_{n})={c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}
are treated as continuous parameters. With this approximation, the evolution of the system from a state
\rho(t_{0})
to
\rho(t_{N})
is

\big|\rho(t_{N})\big\rangle=\Bigg[\prod_{n=1}^{N}\exp{\bigg[-i\Big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}+i\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}\Big)\Delta t\bigg]}\Bigg]\big|\rho(t_{0})\big\rangle

(61)

where the product must be
time-ordered
and the time interval
\Delta t
. Time intervals considered in the remainder of this thesis will be the same for each time slice, although this is for convenience and not a restriction of the piecewise constant approximation.

Although the physical arrangement of the control vector
\bm{c}
is as
K
rows of control sequences vectors, the design of an optimisation algorithm is simpler with a single vector representing the many variables of its scale objective function. For this reason, the control vector is concatenated to column vector when used within the optimiser:

\bm{c}=\begin{pmatrix}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1}^{\vphantom{|}}&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,2}^{\vphantom{|}}&\cdots&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,1}^{\vphantom{|}}&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,2}^{\vphantom{|}}&\cdots&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,1}^{\vphantom{|}}&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,2}^{\vphantom{|}}&\cdots&{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,1}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\vdots\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,1}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\hline\cr[1pt/1.5pt]{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,2}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,2}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\vdots\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,2}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\hline\cr[1pt/1.5pt]\\
\vdots\\
\\
\hline\cr[1pt/1.5pt]{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\\
\vdots\\
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,N}^{\vphantom{|}}\end{pmatrix}

(62)

Although the order
(k,n)
is used here, the ordering is arbitrary and could equally be ordered
(n,k)
as long as the vectorisation of the control amplitudes is consistent within the optimiser.

§ 3  The Pontryagin maximum principle

To investigate the gradient that would be needed for numerical optimisation, in
section

1
, a principle from optimal control theory needs to be imposed, called the
Pontryagin maximum principle

[
246
,
92
]
. It follows from the classical Lagrangian mechanics
[
174
]
, which introduces a
Lagrangian
,
\mathcal{L}(t,\rho(t),\dot{\rho}(t))
, – a function that characterises the dynamics a system. In this case, the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
is defined by the variables of time
t
, the state of the system
\rho(t)
, and the equation of motion of the state
\dot{\rho}(t)
. Lagrangian mechanics is the calculus of variations, and so too is optimal control.

The Pontryagin maximum principle sets an optimal control problem in a similar formulation, with the modification of replacing the equation of motion with the system controls, giving the Lagrangian as
\mathcal{L}(t,\rho(t),{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t))
and the equation of motion depends on the controls. In addition to its Lagrangian formulation, optimal control is also the application of mathematical optimisation, and its solutions are set out by the conditions of Pontryagin maximum principle.

Physical limitations of the instrumentation and constraints on control variable are part of the optimal control problem, and should be formulated within the problem
[
298
]
. In the context of magnetic resonance, the optimal control problem is set out as
[
287
,
77
,
176
]
:

\displaystyle\max_{{c}(t)}{\big(\mathcal{L}\big)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big({c}(t)\big)\triangleq\mathcal{J}{}\big(\rho(T)\big)+\int\limits_{0}^{T}\mathcal{K}\big(t,\rho(t),{c}(t)\big)\dd t

(63)

where the scalar function
\mathcal{J}{}\big(\rho(T)\big)
is a
terminal cost
depending only on the final state of the system at a time
T
, and the integral of the scalar function
\mathcal{K}\big(t,\rho(t),{c}(t)\big)
is a
running cost
which depends on the controls and the state of the system during the time interval
[0,T]
. The controls,
{c}(t)
, affect the equation of motion of the system – the Liouville-von Neumann equation of
eq.

57

\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\big|\rho(t)\big\rangle=-i\Bigg[\int\limits_{0}^{T}\!\!\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}\big({c}(t)\big)\dd t\Bigg]\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle

(64)

where
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}(t)
can be separated as a bilinear system in
eq.

58
.

In analogy to
Lagrange multipliers
of Lagrangian mechanics
[
174
]
a variable called the
adjoint state
(also known as the
costate
) is introduced to solve the optimal control problem. Without exploring the dry depths of Lagrangian mechanics, the
adjoint state
will be introduced by example.

The formal mathematical description of the adjoint states will follow, but it is useful to show an interpretation of their physical significance with an example of state-to-state transfer in
fig.

4
. The state of the system at each time slice forms a state trajectory over a time interval
[0,T]
, containing information of the basis state population at each time increment. If propagated from a defined initial state
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
with
eq.

76
, the control pulses form a unique trajectory of the system under the influence of the drift.

Figure 4:
An example of two state vector trajectories, and the corresponding adjoint state trajectories. The state vector propagates forward in time from the initial condition, under the influence of drift and calculated controls. The adjoint state propagates backwards in time from the desired state, under the influence of the time-reversed drift and controls. This example shows polarisation transfer from a hydrogen spin to a coupled carbon spin (
140
Hz). The two trajectories show polarisation local to each of the spins. In this case, nearly 80% of the polarisation has been transferred with calculated control pulses (not shown).

fig.

4
shows a projection of the state of the system at each time interval onto a representation of magnetisation population local to each spin. The aim is to transfer magnetisation from one spin to the other in a coupled two-spin system with calculated control pulses. It is easy to see the population gradually transfers from one spin to the other over the time interval
[0,T]
.

The state trajectory is the trajectory formed by propagating the system from a defined initial state under the effect of control pulses. The
adjoint state
also forms a trajectory, propagated under the influence of the same drift and the same controls. However, the adjoint state is a propagation backwards in time from the desired final state of the system. The
adjoint state
trajectory is the control problem in reverse.

When an optimal trajectory is found, the state trajectory should end at the desired final state, and the adjoint state should start at the defined initial state of the system. This can be seen in
fig.

4
, where an optimal trajectory has not yet been found; there is a population difference between the adjoint state trajectory and the state trajectory at times
t=0
and at times
t=T
. The adjoint state
\big|\chi(T)\big\rangle=\big|\sigma\big\rangle
, and the state
\big|\rho(0)\big\rangle=\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
form the boundary conditions for the optimal control problem.

The
Pontryagin maximum principle
requires a number of conditions to be met for an optimal trajectory to be found. Just as in the area of numerical optimisation of
section

1
, necessary conditions of the first and second derivatives must be met (
eqs.

7
,
8
), in this case applied to the function called a
pseudo-Hamiltonian
20
20

20

The term
pseudo-Hamiltonian
is introduced to distinguish it from Hamiltonians of quantum mechanics. The
pseudo-Hamiltonian
is a canonical system and therefore it is a Hamiltonian function
[
298
,
92
]

[
77
,
176
]
, derived from
eq.

63
and the
adjoint state

[
246
,
92
]
:

\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{p}}\big(\rho(t),{c}(t)\big)\triangleq\big\langle\chi(t)\big|-i\Bigg[\int\limits_{0}^{T}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}\big({c}(t)\big)\dd t\Bigg]\big|\rho(t)\big\rangle+\mathcal{K}\big(\rho(t),{c}(t)\big)

(65)

The first and second order optimality conditions of
eqs.

7
,
8
, now become

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{p}}}{\partial{c}(t)}=0

(66a)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{p}}}{\partial{c}(t)^{2}}>0

(66b)

in the vicinity of a maximum. The
Pontryagin maximum principle

[
246
]
also requires consideration of the relationship between the state trajectory and the adjoint state trajectory. These give the further necessary conditions

\displaystyle\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\big|\chi(t)\big\rangle=-\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{p}}}{\partial\,|\rho(t)\rangle}

(67a)

\displaystyle\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\big|\rho(t)\big\rangle=\frac{\partial\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{p}}}{\partial\,\langle\chi(t)|}

(67b)

which formally sets out the conclusions made in discussion of
fig.

4
above: as the state trajectory approaches as maximum, the state trajectory as a time
t=T
should completely overlap with the desired target state, and that the adjoint state at a time
t=0
should overlap completely with the initial state of the system.

The formulation of
eq.

63
admits three types of optimal control problem
[
298
,
45
]
:
The Lagrange problem
has only a running cost;
The Mayer problem
has only a terminal cost;
The Bolza problem
both the running cost and the terminal cost are included. The Mayer problem, where the final state of the system forms the optimisation problem, will be the main consideration of this thesis until
chapter

6
, when control amplitude penalties are included in a Bolza problem.

For an unconstrained optimal control problem
\mathcal{K}=0

[
77
]
and the optimal control problem reduces to one of the Mayer problem. The
terminal cost

\mathcal{J}{}
in
eq.

63
will be the focus of the next section on
fidelity
measures.

§ 4  Fidelity measures

Optimal solutions can be found numerically, by optimising
fidelity
– the overlap between the final state of the system and the desired destination state
[
99
,
202
,
154
]
. The optimum of the fidelity functional
\mathcal{J}(c)
with respect to the control sequences
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\vphantom{|}}(t)
in
eq.

58
, subjected to experimental constraints of feasible amplitudes and frequencies, is calculated with
optimal control theory

[
246
,
191
]
. For Hermitian states, the fidelity function is

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}\triangleq\big\langle\sigma\big|\rho(T)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}\in[-1,+1]

(68)

where angular bracket denotes column-wise vectorization of the corresponding density matrix,
\big|\rho(T)\big\rangle
is the final state,
\big|\sigma\big\rangle
is the desired destination state. Finding the optimal value of this inner product, is that of finding its maximum.

Non-Hermitian states can be accounted for with two further definitions of the fidelity functional, either by using only the real part of the overlap in
eq.

68

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}\triangleq\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\sigma\big|\rho(T)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}\in[-1,+1]

(69)

or by using the modulus square
21
21

21

The modulus square of a complex valued vector is
\big|\mathbf{v}\big|^{2}=\mathbf{v}\mathbf{v}^{\ast}
, where
∗
denotes the complex conjugate.
of the overlap in
eq.

68

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}\triangleq\Big|\big\langle\sigma\big|\rho(T)\big\rangle^{\vphantom{2}}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}\in[0,+1]

(70)

The definition of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
allows the optimisation to span a space from that parallel to, orthogonal to, and anti-parallel to
\sigma
. The definition of
\mathcal{J}_{2}
is phase insensitive. For the fidelity to have this physical meaning, the initial state and the system,
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
, and the desired target state,
\big|\sigma\big\rangle
, should be be normalised.

In addition to these state-to-state definitions of fidelity, similar measures can be designed for multi-state mapping:

\mathcal{J}_{\textsc{ur}}=\frac{1}{q_{\max}}\mathrm{Re}\Big(\sum\limits_{q=1}^{q_{\max}}\big\langle\sigma_{q}\big|\rho_{q}(T)\big\rangle\Big)

(71)

where
\sigma_{k}
define the desired state propagated in time from corresponding initial states
\rho_{k}(0)
respectively. When
eq.

71
is averaged over 3 state-to-state problems, together completely characterising a rotation of the state in Cartesian coordinates, the optimal control problem becomes a universal rotation in 3D e.g.

\begin{matrix}\big|\rho_{1}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{z}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{x}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{1}\big\rangle\\
\big|\rho_{2}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{x}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{z}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{2}\big\rangle\\
\big|\rho_{3}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{y}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{y}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{3}\big\rangle\end{matrix}

These 3 state-to-state problems can restated with a fidelity definition to find an
effective propagator

[
154
]
or an
effective Hamiltonian

[
310
]
over the time period of, and subject to, the control sequence. A measure of the distance between two matrices can be defined by the the desired propagation over the total time period
[0,T]
.

The fidelity definition for this desired
effective propagator
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{3}\triangleq\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}_{D}\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!\,(T)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{3}\in[-1,+1]

(72)

where
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}_{D}=\exp{\big(-i\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{D}T\big)}
is the desired effective propagator for the effective Hamiltonian
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{D}
. In analogy to
eq.

70
, the modulus square of this overlap is

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{4}\triangleq\Big|\big\langle\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}_{D}\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!\,(T)\big\rangle^{\vphantom{2}}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{4}\in[0,+1]

(73)

In
eqs.

72
,
73
, the overlap of two propagators is defined by the Frobenius inner product as the trace of the operator product:

\big\langle\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}_{D}\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!\,(T)\big\rangle\triangleq\Tr\big(\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{U}}}}}_{D}^{\dagger}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!\,(T)\big)

(74)

Although not used in this thesis, the definitions of fidelity in
eqs.

72
,
73
are reported to avoid local minima which
eq.

71
encounter
[
154
,
310
]
.

§ 5  Fidelity gradient

To make use of Newton-type methods of
section

1
, a gradient of the fidelity functional with respect to the control amplitudes is required. The first step to achieve this is to expand the fidelity definitions in
eqs.

68
,
69
,
70
. As a function of control amplitudes, the fidelity definitions become

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(\bm{c}_{n}^{\vphantom{|}})=\big\langle\sigma\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N-1}\cdots\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{2}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{1}\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle

(75a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{1}(\bm{c}_{n}^{\vphantom{|}})=\mathrm{Re}\big\langle\sigma\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N-1}\cdots\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{2}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{1}\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}(\bm{c}_{n}^{\vphantom{|}})=\Big|\big\langle\sigma\big|\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N-1}\cdots\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{2}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{1}\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle^{\vphantom{2}}\Big|^{2}

Once the controllable part of the Hamiltonian becomes parametrised, the variation
{\delta\mathcal{J}}/{\delta\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}}

becomes an instance of a non-linear optimisation problem for a continuous function in

Elements of the gradient are the derivative of the fidelity functional with respect to the control amplitudes
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}
. The only dependence of the fidelity on the control amplitudes is in the propagator at that time slice,
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{n}
– calculation of the time propagator derivatives will be set out in
section

1
.

Once the
K\times N
propagator derivatives are calculated, they can be used to give the derivative of the fidelity functional with respect to the control amplitude. From the previous section
section

3
, the fidelity at a time slice is the overlap, the inner product, of state of the system and its adjoint state at that time slice.

The state trajectory can be calculated from one forward-time propagation, and the adjoint trajectory from one backward-time propagation. The forward propagation from
\rho_{0}
to
\rho_{n}
is

\big|\rho_{n}\big\rangle=\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{n}\cdots\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{2}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{1}\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle

(78)

and the backward propagation from
\sigma
to
\rho_{n+1}
is

\big|\chi_{n}\big\rangle=\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{n}^{\,\,\dagger}\cdots\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N-1}^{\,\,\dagger}\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}}}\!_{N}^{\,\,\dagger}\big|\sigma\big\rangle

A particular strength of the
grape
method is that the gradient of the fidelity functional has the same complexity as the fidelity functional
[

,
154
,
311
,

,

]
and reduces to finding the derivative of a propagator at each time slice and for each control. Calculation of these derivatives will be the focus of

The elements of the gradient should be arranged as a vector of derivatives with respect to control amplitudes,
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}
, for a control
k)
at a time steps

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}(\bm{c})=\begin{pmatrix}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1}^{\vphantom{|}})&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,2}^{\vphantom{|}})&\cdots&\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}({c}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,N}^{\vphantom{|}})\\

During the forward-time propagation of the state
\rho_{0}
with the propagators in
eq.

76
, a trajectory of the system dynamics is produced. This can be stored in an array of state vectors,
\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{0}&\rho_{1}&\cdots&\rho_{N}\end{pmatrix}
, and used for trajectory analysis of the controlled system dynamics
[
166
]
.

§ 6  Algorithm

An effective optimisation should be preconditioned
[
229
]
, which means that the optimisation variables should not be too small or too large. For the
grape
method this means that the control amplitudes should be normalised, requiring a nominal power level to be separated from the control amplitudes

\bm{c}\to p_{c}\tilde{\bm{c}}

(82)

where
p_{c}
is a control power level

The
grape
algorithm can be summarised in the following way:

1

Define the system uncontrollable Hamiltonian
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{0}
, relaxation
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{R}}}}}
, and control operators
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{k}
.

2

Set an initial state
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
, and target state
\big|\sigma\big\rangle
of the system.

3

Start with an initial guess
22
22

22

Can be a random guess
of control amplitudes,
\tilde{\bm{c}}
. and nominal power level
p_{c}
with
\max{|\tilde{\bm{c}}}|\approx+1
and
|\min{\tilde{\bm{c}}}|\approx 0
.

4

Call
grape
as the objective function and multiply the control amplitudes by the nominal power level.

5

Propagate the system forward in time from
\big|\rho_{0}\big\rangle
with the time propagators in
eq.

76
and store the states at each time slice in
eq.

78

6

Propagate the system backward in time from
\rho_{0}
with the complex conjugate of the time propagators in
eq.

76
and store the states at each time slice in
eq.

79

7

Calculate gradient elements with
eq.

80b
and the stored states from
eqs.

78
,
79
.

8

Multiply the gradient vector with
p_{c}
and send back to the optimiser.

9

Update the control amplitudes with an update rule in
eq.

49
.

10

Test termination conditions, and an optimal solution hasn’t been found, return to
item

4

Hessian Calculations

“A man of genius makes no mistakes. His errors are volitional and are the portals of discovery.”

– James Joyce,
Ulysses

A method of optimal control, called
grape
, was reviewed in
section

2
with application to magnetic resonance systems of quantum ensembles in
chapter

2
. Applying rapidly converging numerical optimisation methods of
section

1
to optimal control algorithms will be the focus of this chapter. The Newton-Raphson version of
grape
(Newton-
grape
) has the potential to achieve quadratic convergence, compared to the superlinear convergence of state-of-the-art methods currently used in this area of optimal control, as outlined in
chapter

1
.

Scientific instruments used in many applications of quantum theory have reached the limits of what is physically, legally or financially possible. Examples include power deposition safeguards in
mri
instruments
[
49
]
, sample heating thresholds in biomolecular
nmr
spectroscopy
[
110
]
and the steep dependence of the cost of superconducting magnets on the induction they generate
[
133
]
. Some limits, such as the length of time a patient can be persuaded to stay inside an
mri
machine, are psychological
[
260
]
, but in practice no less real. In these time-constrained situations faster convergence and greater code parallelisation, compared to other quantum control algorithms in the
grape
family, have the prospect of giving financial and medical benefits.

This chapter demonstrates that the Hessian of the
grape
fidelity functional is unusually cheap, having the same asymptotic complexity scaling as the functional itself. This leads to the possibility of using very efficient numerical optimisation techniques. In particular, the Newton-Raphson method with a regularised and well-conditioned Hessian (
sections

1
,
2
,
3
) is shown to require fewer system trajectory evaluations than any other algorithm in the
grape
family. The methods presented in this chapter were published by Goodwin and Kuprov in
[
108
]
and are implemented in
Spinach
v2.0 and later
1
1

1

Available at
www.spindynamics.org
.
and results are presented in
section

3
.

§ 1  Fidelity Hessian

The Newton-Raphson method in
section

2
requires a Hessian matrix so that local curvature information can be used to improve a search direction compared to a gradient-based method. From
eq.

15
, the Newton-step search direction of an iteration
\jmath
leads the update of the control amplitudes to maximise a fidelity function

\bm{c}^{(\jmath+1)}=\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}+\varepsilon\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\Big]^{-1}\Big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\Big\rangle

(1)

where the step length is
\varepsilon=1
for a Newton-step, or with
\varepsilon
calculated with a line search method, such as those in
section

4
.

Figure 1:
Diagram of the block structure of the Hessian matrix (here, with
K=4
and
N=5
).
a
shows the diagonal proper, with
\big(K\times N\big)
elements calculated with
eqs.

5a
,
5b
,
5c
.
b
shows the off-diagonal elements within the block diagonal, with
\big(K(K-1)\times N\big)
elements calculated with
eqs.

6a
,
6b
,
6c
.
c
shows the block off-diagonal elements of
eq.

4
, with
\big(K^{2}\times N(N-1)\big)
elements calculated with
eqs.

7a
,
7b
,
7c
.

§ 1  Hessian structure

The fidelity Hessian with more than one control is a tensor, and should be arranged as a block matrix of
N\times N
blocks of
K\times K
matrices, consistent with
eqs.

62
,
81
:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}(\bm{c})\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{2}&\mathbf{D}_{1,2}&&\cdots&&\mathbf{D}_{1,N}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\mathbf{D}_{2,1}&\mathbf{D}_{2}^{2}&&\cdots&&\mathbf{D}_{2,N}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\ddots&&\vdots\vskip 5.0pt\\
\mathbf{D}_{N,1}&\mathbf{D}_{N,2}&&\cdots&&\mathbf{D}_{N}^{2}\end{pmatrix}

(2)

where the matrices
\mathbf{D}_{m,n}
are each of size
K\times K
. The block diagonals, depicted in
fig.

1

a
–
b
, are

\mathbf{D}_{n}^{2}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}^{\,2}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}^{\,2}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\vskip 5.0pt\\
\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}^{\,2}}\end{pmatrix}

(3)

and the block off-diagonals depicted in
fig.

1

c
are

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{m,n}\triangleq\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}}\vskip 5.0pt\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\vskip 5.0pt\\
\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,n}}&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,n}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,m}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle K,n}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle n\neq m

(4)

§ 2  Hessian elements

The second derivatives of the fidelity
[
5
]
have a similar form to the gradient elements in
eqs.

80a
,

in between the two first-order propagator derivatives.
.

eqs.

5a
,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

show the Hessian has almost the same numerical computational complexity scaling as the fidelity functional in
eqs.

68
,
69
,
70

[
5
]
, just as the gradient does in
eqs.

80a
,

,
80c

[
154
]
. This situation is highly unusual in non-linear optimization theory – Hessians are normally so expensive that a significant body of work exists on the subject of avoiding their calculation and recovering second derivative information in an approximate way from the gradient history
[
79
,
229
,
194
,
34
]
. The recent
bfgs-grape
algorithm
[
86
]
is an example of such approach. The fact that the Hessian is relatively cheap suggests Newton-Raphson type algorithms
[
79
,
229
]
with the control sequence update rule of
eq.

1
.

However, three logistical problems present themselves that must be solved before the method becomes useful in practice:

1

Efficient calculation of second derivatives of the propagators in
eq.

76
. This problem will be considered in detail in
chapter

5
.

2

Propagator derivative recycling between gradient elements in
eqs.

80a
,
80b
,
80c
and block off-diagonal Hessian elements in
eqs.

7a
,
7b
,
7c
, required for efficient scaling. Outlined in
[
107
]
, the latency of the cache storage device means that for small matrices it may be faster to recalculate the function. A caching procedure becomes beneficial for larger matrices.

3

Regularisation and conditioning of the Hessian matrix in
eq.

1
is needed to avoid the numerical difficulties associated with its inverse – a Hessian matrix should be positive definite, from
eq.

9
, and well-conditioned
[
79
,
229
]
. This will be the main focus of the remainder of this chapter.

§ 3  Control operator commutativity

The full Hessian requires
(K\times N)
second-order propagator derivative evaluations of
eqs.

5a
,
5b
,
5c
and
fig.

1

a
. It was pointed out that the block off-diagonal second-order propagator derivatives of
eqs.

7a
,
7b
,
7c
and
fig.

1

c
are essentially free, having already been calculated with a gradient evaluation. There is a further useful property of the Hessian matrix: that it should be real and symmetric. Potentially, this can halve the number of calculations of the type in
eqs.

6a
,
6b
,
6c
.

However, it is noted in
[
195
,
211
]
that the additive law fails unless there is commutativity:

\displaystyle\e^{t\mathbf{A}}\e^{t\mathbf{B}}=\e^{t(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B})},

\displaystyle\text{iff}\qquad\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}

(8)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}}=\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}},

\displaystyle\text{iff}\quad\Big[\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\Big]=0

(9)

The relation is only valid if the associated control operators commute with each other. To ensure the Hessian matrix is symmetric
3
3

3

When a Hessian is not symmetric, its eigenvalues are not real.
when condition of
eq.

9
is not met, the following formula must be used

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}({{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n},{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n})\\
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}({{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n},{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n})\end{cases}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg(\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}}+\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}}\Bigg),

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad\Big[\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k},\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\Big]\neq 0

(10)

which requires two second-order propagator derivative evaluations.

§ 2  Hessian Regularisation

Maximisation with Newton-Raphson and quasi-Newton methods relies on the necessary conditions for the truncated Taylor series approximation to be valid. This was introduced using a local quadratic approximation of the objective function in
eq.

13
, restated here for the fidelity functional:

\Delta\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath+1)}\big)-\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\approx\Big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\Big|\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\Big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\Big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\Big|\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\Big\rangle

(11)

In addition to the gradient being zero in the vicinity of a stationary point
\bm{c}^{(\ast)}
,

\lim_{\bm{c}\to\bm{c}^{(\ast)}}\Big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\ast)}\big)\Big\|_{\infty}=0

(12)

the second-order sufficient condition of
eq.

9
must also be true in the vicinity of a stationary point:

\displaystyle\lim_{\bm{c}\to\bm{c}^{(\ast)}}\Big\langle\bm{c}\Big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\ast)}\big)\Big|\bm{c}\Big\rangle>0,

\displaystyle\forall\bm{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K\cdot N)}

(13)

i.e. the Hessian must be positive definite near a stationary point
\bm{c}^{(\ast)}
. This is evident from
eq.

1
, in which a negative definite Hessian would result in a step being performed down, rather than up, the corresponding gradient direction. Furthermore, an indefinite Hessian is an indication of a saddle point, shown in
fig.

1

c
, which should be avoided during maximisation of the fidelity functional. Forcing a Hessian to be a definite matrix ensures that steps are made that avoid saddle points. Forcing a positive definite Hessian, as opposed to a negative definite Hessian, ensures that a step is always made in the correct ascent/descent direction.

A significant problem is that, far away from a stationary point, the Hessian is not expected to be positive definite
[
238
,
237
,
239
,
85
,
251
,
296
]
. Small Hessian eigenvalues are also problematic because they result in overly long steps that can be detrimental because most fidelity functionals are not actually quadratic. A significant amount of research has gone into modifying the Hessian in such a way as to avoid these undesired behaviours
[
102
,
112
,
16
,
121
,
101
,
40
,
214
,
281
,
215
,
15
,
14
]
.

§ 1  Cholesky factorisation

One fairly cheap way to work around an indefinite Hessian is to attempt
Cholesky factorisation

[
96
]
, which exists for any invertible positive definite matrix
[
106
]
:

\displaystyle\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\text{T}}

(14)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big[\mathbf{L}^{-1}\Big]^{\text{T}}\mathbf{L}^{-1}

(15)

where
\mathbf{L}
is a lower triangular matrix with positive diagonal elements and
\mathbf{L}^{\text{T}}
is its transpose
4
4

4

The argument of the Hessian has been dropped here, and for the rest of the chapter. It should be assumed that within equations and definitions, the Hessian, gradient, and controls are all of the same iteration,
\jmath
, unless stated otherwise:

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\to\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}

\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\to\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}

\displaystyle\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\to\bm{c}

. If a Cholesky factorisation finds solution, the Hessian is positive definite. If a Cholesky factorisation fails, the Hessian is indefinite, and an identity matrix may be used as a substitute for the Hessian, effectively reverting to a gradient ascent step for any iterations that produce an indefinite Hessian
[
105
]
.

The problem with this approach is that indefinite Hessians become more common as the dimension of the problem increases, making the maximiser spend most of the time in the gradient ascent mode and destroying any advantage of the second-order method over simple gradient ascent – this technique is not recommended.

However, there is a further use of the Cholesky factorisation test for an indefinite Hessian: an algorithm can be designed to iteratively test the Hessian, modifying the Hessian at each stage. Using the Cholesky decomposition on a Hessian
[
96
]
with a multiple of the unit matrix added
[
102
,
121
,
214
,
215
]
:

\displaystyle\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]+\sigma\bm{\mathbbm{1}}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\text{T}},

\displaystyle\sigma\geqslant 0

(16)

The choice of
\sigma
is made to produce a positive definite Hessian satisfying
eq.

13
, with the trial value

\displaystyle\sigma=\begin{cases}\Big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big\|_{\text{F}}-\min\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{ii}\vskip 10.00002pt\\
\Big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big\|_{\text{F}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\min\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{ii}<0\\
\min\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{ii}\geqslant 0\end{array}

chosen in that way because the Frobenius norm of the Hessian is an upper bound on the largest absolute eigenvalue. The value of
\sigma
is increased iteratively until the Cholesky decomposition succeeds
[
229
]
and the inverse Hessian may be obtained.

A benefits of this iterative Cholesky factorisation method are that an inverse Hessian is automatically produced from
eq.

15
, which is a simple task to compute for invertible triangular matrices
[
106
]
, and that the method avoids the expensive operation of calculating an eigendecomposition, which is time-consuming for large Hessian matrices.

§ 2  Trust Region Method

A more sophisticated workaround is to use the eigenvalue shifting method, also called the trust region method, adapted from the Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm
[
204
,
95
]
. Hessian eigenvalues may be computed explicitly
[
112
]

\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}

(19)

where
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues of
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}
and
\mathbf{Q}
is the matrix with columns of corresponding eigenvectors. A precise estimate can now be made for the largest negative eigenvalue of the Hessian, and added to the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues to reform the Hessian from this modified eigendecomposition:

\displaystyle\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{\text{reg}}\!\!\!=\mathbf{Q}\big(\mathbf{\Lambda}+\sigma\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)\mathbf{Q}^{-1},

\displaystyle\sigma=\max\big(0,\delta-\lambda_{\min}\big)

(20)

where
\lambda_{\min}
is the minimum eigenvalue of the Hessian,
\min(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{ii})
. The arbitrary positive value of
\delta
is included to make the Hessian positive definite. The primary problem with this method is that, for poorly conditioned or near-singular Hessian matrices, the regularisation procedure destroys much of the curvature information and the technique effectively becomes a combination of the Newton-Raphson and gradient ascent methods. Formally,
eqs.

19
,
20
do solve the step direction problem, but practical testing indicates the convergence rate can fail to achieve true quadratic convergence near a maximum.

§ 3  Rational Function Optimisation

A refined regularisation method, called rational function optimization
[
16
,
281
,
15
]
, does allow a modified Hessian to be a well-conditioned, positive definite matrix. It replaces the truncated Taylor series in
eq.

11
with a
[2/1]
Padé approximant
[
234
,
127
]
, defined in
section

2
, approximating as a rational function:

\Delta\mathcal{J}=\frac{\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|\,\bm{c}\big\rangle+\dfrac{1}{2}\big\langle\bm{c}\,\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big|\,\bm{c}\big\rangle}{1+\big\langle\bm{c}\,\big|\,\mathbf{S}\,\big|\,\bm{c}\big\rangle}

(21)

This preserves the derivative information of
eq.

11
, leaving the necessary conditions unchanged, because the derivatives of
\big[1+\big\langle\bm{c}\big|\mathbf{S}\big|\bm{c}\big\rangle\big]^{-1}
give contributions only through higher orders in
\bm{c}
. The nature of the rational function means that the asymptotes of
\Delta\mathcal{J}
and is gradients remain finite for
\bm{c}\to\pm\infty
, determined by the Hessian and the symmetric scaling matrix
\mathbf{S}
. The first-order necessary condition for
eq.

12
gives the following eigenvalue equation:

\begin{pmatrix}\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big]&\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle\\
\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{c}\\
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=2\Delta\mathcal{J}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{S}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{c}\\
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(22)

Choosing a uniform scaling matrix
\mathbf{S}=\alpha^{-2}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}

[
15
]
, where
0<\alpha\leqslant 1
, reduces this equation to

\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{2}\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big]&\alpha\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle\\
\alpha\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{c}}/\alpha\;\\
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=2\Delta\mathcal{J}\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{c}}/\alpha\;\\
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(23)

Rational function optimisation proceeds in a similar way to eigenvalue shifting methods described above, except the shifting is applied to the augmented Hessian matrix. Defining the eigendecomposition of the augmented Hessian matrix as

\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]^{\text{aug}}\!\!\!=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{2}\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big]&\alpha\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle\\
\alpha\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}

(24)

a similar shifting formula to
eq.

20
can be used to ensure a positive definite augmented Hessian matrix

\displaystyle\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{\text{reg}}^{\text{aug}}\!\!\!=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\mathbf{Q}\big(\mathbf{\Lambda}+\sigma\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)\mathbf{Q}^{-1},

\displaystyle\sigma=\max\big(0,-\lambda_{\min}\big)

(25)

The top left corner block of the regularised augmented Hessian is then used for the Newton-Raphson step
[
16
,
281
,
15
]
in
eq.

1
.

§ 4  Hessian Conditioning

Motivated by the
Wolfe conditions
of
eqs.

52
,
54
,
55

[
333
,
334
]
, it may be necessary to place restrictions on the search direction by excluding directions near-orthogonal to the steepest ascent vector. This condition, called the
angle criterion
, states that for any angle
\theta^{(\jmath)}
between the search direction,
\bm{d}^{\vphantom{|}}_{s}
, and the gradient vector should be bounded away from orthogonality. Using the derivation set out by Fletcher
[
79
]
, the critical angle is should be

\displaystyle\theta^{(\jmath)}\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}-\mu

\displaystyle\forall\jmath

(26)

with
\mu>0
and independent of
\jmath
, and the angle
\theta^{(\jmath)}
is calculated as

\displaystyle\cos\big(\theta^{(\jmath)}\big)=\frac{\Big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\Big|\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\Big\rangle}{\big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big(\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big)\big\|_{2}\cdot\big\|\bm{c}^{(\jmath)}\big\|_{2}}

(27)

For Newton-type methods with a continuous gradient, using inexact line searches of
eqs.

52
,
54
,
55
, maximisation is bounded from above. This leads to the conclusion that, as the change in fidelity tends to zero,
\Delta\mathcal{J}\to 0
, then so does the slope
\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|\bm{c}\big\rangle\to 0
. Two cases remain: either
\vphantom{+}{}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}{}^{(\jmath)}\to 0
or
\mathcal{J}{}^{(\jmath)}\to\infty

[
79
]
.

The condition number defined here as the ratio of its largest Hessian eigenvalue,
\max(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{ii})
, to the smallest Hessian eigenvalue,
\min(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{ii})
:

\zeta=\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}

(28)

The sufficient condition for ascent is that the Hessian is positive definite. This gives a sufficient condition for the angle criterion of
eqs.

26
,
27
, requiring the condition number of the Hessian matrix also to be bounded above. With the inequalities

\displaystyle\big\|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\rangle\big\|_{2}\leqslant\lambda_{\max}

(29)

\displaystyle\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle\geqslant\lambda_{\min}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle

(30)

the sufficient condition for the angle criterion is

\theta^{(\jmath)}\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{(\jmath)}}

(31)

With this condition satisfied, the Armijo condition in
eq.

52
is also satisfied. The methods that follow will ensure that the condition number of the Hessian matrix is bounded so the above conditions are met.

The condition number of the Hessian should be bound; one way to achieve this is to allow the scaling constant
\alpha
in
eqs.

24
,
25
to vary according to the condition of the Hessian. The value of
\alpha
is reduced until the condition number becomes acceptable (nearer to
\alpha=1
, when the Hessian is well-conditioned), for example:

\displaystyle\alpha_{r+1}=\phi\alpha_{r}

while

\displaystyle\zeta>\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{\textsc{eps}}}

(32)

where
eps
is machine precision and
\alpha_{0}=1
. The cube-root of machine precision reflects the allowable condition number for an acceptable cubic interpolation within the sectioning phase of a line search in
section

4
. The factor
0<\phi<1
is used to iteratively decrease the condition number of the Hessian – this is the method used to condition the Hessian in the examples presented below. It should be noted that a value
\phi>1
may be used to increase the condition number of the Hessian when it is very small and the inequality of
eq.

32
is reversed.

Quantum mechanics is rich in situations that produce very small values of Hessian and gradient elements. Choosing
\alpha_{0}
to be the value that would shift the smallest eigenvalues to be above
1
, giving a similar effect to choosing
\delta=1
in
eq.

20
, accounts for optimisation problems that are inherently ill-conditioned in this way. When the choice

\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\big|\lambda_{\min}\big|}}

(33)

is made, the augmented Hessian in
eqs.

24
,
25
becomes

\displaystyle\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]^{\text{aug}}\!\!\!=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}}\big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\big]&\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\big|\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big\rangle\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}}}\big\langle\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla\!\mathcal{J}\big|&\mathbf{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}^{-1}

(34)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccr}&\Big[\vphantom{+}^{\vphantom{|}}_{\vphantom{|}}\nabla^{2}\!\mathcal{J}\Big]_{\text{reg}}^{\text{aug}}\!\!\!=\lambda_{\min}\mathbf{Q}\big(\mathbf{\Lambda}+\sigma\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)\mathbf{Q}^{-1},&&\sigma=\max\big(0,-\lambda_{\min}\big)\end{array}

Practical testing indicates that this combination of using initial scaling, then accepting large condition numbers for the Hessian, allows the Newton-Raphson method to avoid getting stuck at inflection points. The condition number would grow to a large value around the inflection point and then shrink back when the point has been avoided. Due to the tendency of the Hessian to increase condition number as it approaches the maximiser, machine precision could eventually become a limit to this type of conditioning. To avoid a slowdown at the final stages of the optimisation, an upper bound is placed on the
\alpha
parameter in
eq.

32
– this guarantees that the terminal convergence is always quadratic.

In practice, at each optimisation step the function code attempts to compute the Cholesky decomposition of
eq.

14
. If that is successful then no regularisation is needed, otherwise the function proceeds to regularise with the methods described above. Once the ascent direction and the initial step length are obtained from
eq.

1
it is advantageous to perform a line search procedure.

Converged Hessian matrices, arranged
N\times K
(opposite to
fig.

to clearly see the time evolution of control channels,
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{x}^{(\text{H})}
and
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{y}^{(\text{H})}
, produced by one of the random seeds in
figs.

,

.
b
Newton method almost give a multiple of
\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
,
a
has structure – indicating an indefinite matrix from the Newton method and a positive definite matrix from the Quasi-Newton method (the
bfgs
method has a single iteration of the Cholesky regularisation).

§ 3  Convergence analysis

In comparing the performance of this Newton-Raphson optimal control, the following optimisation problems will be used
5
5

5

The templates for these optimal control simulations are included in
Spinach
–v2.0, downloadable from
www.spindynamics.org
:

•

State-to-state transfer over a scalar coupled hydrofluorocarbon fragment spin system, as described in
[
108
]
.

•

State-to-state transfer in a quadrupolar
14
N spin, transferring the population between the
T_{1,0}
and
T_{2,2}
states of
eqs.

67
,
77
.

•

Phase-modulated universal rotation pulses with a sine function amplitude profile, and relaxation. The system is a chain of
101
non-interacting
13
C spins spread over
50
kHz.

For fair comparison, all optimisations are run a number of times from different initial guesses
6
6

6

Random numbers were seeded with
rng(32)
at the start of each convergence analysis, and the same starting guesses were used for different methods of optimisation.
, allowing identification of the optimisation convergence characteristics.
