Newton-Raphson methods that need Hessian regularisation need a framework of comparison to current leading
[
86
]
quasi-Newton methods. However, the
\ell
-
bfgs
method does not store an explicit Hessian matrix, only calculating the resulting search direction vector. For the use of testing regularisation methods, standard
bfgs
methods will be used – which should give very similar convergence since the
\ell
-
bfgs
and
bfgs
methods are both rank-2 updates
[
229
]
.

The
dfp
and
bfgs
methods perform a rank-2 update, commonly on the inverse Hessian using
eqs.

36
,
37
, and with a defined positive definite inverse Hessian approximation. However, regularisation methods of
eqs.

20
,
24
,
25
may have other beneficial effects on the Hessian matrix; making the Hessian sufficiently positive definite and well-conditioned. A fair comparison of regularised Newton-Raphson methods should be with quasi-Newton methods which update the Hessian proper with
eq.

37
with the same, forced, regularisation and conditioning as the Newton-Raphson Hessian. Concentrating on the arguably superior rank-2 update of the
bfgs
method,
fig.

5
shows a comparison between the two
bfgs
update formulas in
eqs.

38
,
37
.

The plots of
fig.

5
clearly show there is no appreciable difference of performance or computational cost in the
bfgs
Hessian update and the
bfgs
inverse-Hessian update methods. This justifies the use of a
bfgs
Hessian update of
eq.

37
as a benchmark of comparison using regularised and conditioned Hessian matrices.
fig.

5
should be used as a benchmark for the current best
grape
method with
\ell
-
bfgs
optimisation
[
86
]
.

Correcting an indefinite Hessian with Cholesky factorisation

Initial testing of the Newton-Raphson method failed to proceed further than a single iterate, failing to find a search direction based from an indefinite Hessian matrix. Further to this, forcing a step along the direction of the Newton-step shows the direction is not an ascent direction and an optimisation cannot proceed based on violation of the Wolfe conditions.

An iterative Cholesky factorisation, of
eq.

14
, can be used to correct the non-ascent direction from an indefinite Hessian – forcing the positive definite property, allowing a Newton-Raphson method to proceed. Results of this optimisation are shown compared with the equivalent
bfgs
method, where at least one Cholesky factorisation is forced, in
fig.

6
. Both methods use a bracketing and sectioning line search with cubic interpolation.

Figure 6:
Comparison of optimisation methods for state-to-state transfer in a scalar coupled hydrofluorocarbon fragment, transferring the population between the
\hat{L}_{z}^{\text{(H)}}
and
\hat{L}_{z}^{\text{(F)}}
states (pulse and trajectory shown in
fig.

4
): Newton-
grape
and
bfgs
-
grape
methods are regularised with a Cholesky factorisation (forced in the case of
bfgs
-
grape
). Both methods use a bracketing and sectioning line search.
a
Comparing the iterations count.
b
Comparing the gradient/Hessian calculation count.

The convergence characteristics in
fig.

6
are not promising: linear convergence at best. They show that an iterative Cholesky factorisation does allow the Newton-Raphson method to proceed, finding an ascent direction, correcting an indefinite Hessian. This also shows that regularisation is needed for the Newton-
grape
method.

However, the convergence for the Newton-Raphson method is not quadratic, reverting to the linear convergence of a gradient ascent method. Furthermore, there is a heavy load on the line search, which is indicated by the large amount of gradient calculations in
fig.

6

b
.

Comparison of the Newton method to the
bfgs
method, both with a Cholesky factorisation show that this method of regularisation quickly destroys any useful information from the Hessian and the Hessian approximation – both methods effectively rely on the line search to make progress, with the second order information only showing a vague ascent direction.

The iterative Cholesky factorisation reduces the Newton and
bfgs
methods to a gradient ascent method, which is clearly not adequate for a Newton-Raphson method in
grape
.

Although a Cholesky factorisation is not ideal for regularising a Hessian, it is a cheap way to recognise an indefinite matrix. Within a finished Newton-Raphson method product, the check of its positive definite property should be done with an attempted Cholesky factorisation: if it succeeds then no regularisation is required, if not then the Newton-Raphson method should proceed to a more sophisticated regularisation method.

Correcting an ill-conditioned Hessian with RFO & TRM

Figure 7:
Comparison of optimisation methods for state-to-state transfer in a scalar coupled hydrofluorocarbon fragment, transferring the population between the
\hat{L}_{z}^{\text{(H)}}
and
\hat{L}_{z}^{\text{(F)}}
states (pulse and trajectory shown in
fig.

4
): All methods use a bracketing and sectioning line search.
a
Comparing the iterations count.
b
Comparing the gradient/Hessian calculation count.

As indicated in
section

4
, if the condition number of the Hessian is not bounded, a search direction can get too close to the vector orthogonal to the gradient. One method that can control the condition number is the
trm
of
eq.

20
. Here, a multiple of the identity matrix is added to the Hessian eigendecomposition, shifting all eigenvalues to a defined level above zero. More specifically, making a shift to
\lambda_{\min}=1
ensures that small eigenvalues do not inflate the condition number.

Results in
fig.

7
indicate, in addition to correcting for a non-ascent direction and allowing a Newton-Raphson method to proceed, that a quadratic convergence phase does occur in the vicinity of the maximiser. Comparison of a
trm
conditioned
bfgs
method in
fig.

7
to that without
trm
in
fig.

5
show a slight improvement in convergence, with
\penalty\ 70\%
reduction in optimisation iterations and a large saving in computational cost of
\penalty\ 65\%
reduction in gradient calculations. This indicates that a better search direction is produced with
trm
than without it.

The conditioned Newton-Raphson method cannot be compared fairly with an unconditioned Newton-Raphson method, and it must be inferred that
trm
has a similar effect in finding a good search direction. When comparing the Newton-
trm
with
bfgs
-
trm
in
fig.

7
, the benefits of calculating an explicit Hessian are evident, showing its expected quadratic convergence: Newton-
trm
approaches its quadratic phase in about half the number of iterations and at about half the calculation cost of
bfgs
-
trm
. However, there is a difference in the character of the quadratic phase of the two methods, the Newton-Raphson method follows a much steeper quadratic convergence, and the
bfgs
method follows a quadratic convergence with a similar profile to that of
bfgs
without forced regularisation and conditioning in
fig.

5
.

Figure 8:
Hessian conditioning iterations for Newton-Raphson with
rfo
. Showing how the number of conditioning iterations increases for both optimisation problems of
fig.

11
and
fig.

7
;
a
as the optimisation iterations increase, and
b
as the fidelity decreases.

The iteratively conditioned
rfo
method of
eqs.

34
,
4
including scaling of the Hessian with its corresponding gradient, is the final method compared in
fig.

7
. The Hessian condition number is bound below
10^{4}
(with
\phi=0.9
in
eq.

32
)
7
7

7

Testing find a condition number bound of
\zeta<10^{4}
particularly effective. The theoretical bound of
\zeta<\sqrt[3]{\textsc{eps}}\approx 1.6\times 10^{5}
performs slightly worse in comparison, reaching quadratic convergence a couple of iterations later than with
\zeta<10^{4}
.
. The need to increase the number of conditioning iterations as the maximum is approached can clearly be seen in
fig.

8
, indicating that the Hessian becomes more ill-conditioned the closer it gets to a maximum. The
rfo
methods both outperform their
trm
counterparts in convergence and computational cost. It should also be noted that the
bfgs

rfo
has less computational cost compared to the Newton-Raphson
trm
. This is because the self-scaling provided by the
rfo
greatly reduces the strain on the line search algorithm. Essentially,
rfo
scales the Hessian and gradient, which is usually performed within the line search giving a smaller step size for a correspondingly large condition number of the Hessian.

The Newton-Raphson method with
rfo
regularisation and iterative conditioning has
\penalty\ 85\%
reduction in the computational cost of the current best
bfgs
method in
[
86
]
(when comparing to
fig.

5
), and has a predictable convergence (evident from the small variance from the average convergence trajectories), with a reduction of
\penalty\ 80\%
in optimisation iterations, compared to the
bfgs
method. Further information on the Hessian structure is depicted in
fig.

2
, indicating
rfo
regularisation preserves more of the Hessian structure compared to other regularisation methods.

Figure 9:
Step lengths for optimisations is
fig.

7
,
a
as a function of
1-\mathcal{J}_{1}
, and
b
as a function of iteration count.

Looking deeper into the line search, step lengths at each iterate show how the Newton-Raphson method with
rfo
has the best convergence characteristics of regularisation methods tested. Step lengths at each iterate for those simulations in
fig.

7
are shown in
fig.

9
.
bfgs
and inverse-
bfgs
methods without regularisation have the same spread of step-lengths at each iterate, just as they have the same convergence characteristics in
fig.

5
(not shown here). The general trend in all step length calculations of
fig.

9
is that they start small and converge to
\varepsilon=1
near the maximiser.

Quadratic convergence is reached very quickly in
\penalty\ 10
iterations for the Newton-
grape
method with
rfo
. This is evident from
fig.

9

b
, where the step length
\varepsilon=1
and remains there until a optimum is reached in (to the accuracy of
eps
)
8
8

8

The only time there is true quadratic convergence is when the step length has converged to
1
and remains
1
for the rest of the simulation. This is characteristic of the Newton-Raphson method and is a good indication that Hessian calculations and regularisation calculations have no errors - and the method is indeed a Newton-Raphson method proper.
.

bfgs
methods with regularisation have a similar shape in step length evolution in
fig.

9
to
bfgs
method without regularisation. This improvement is seen at the start of the step-length evolution, approximately the first ten iterations, where step-lengths are larger than those of the
bfgs
methods without regularisation. The regularised
bfgs
methods then follow a similar shape to non-regularised methods, having a similar rate of change per iterate. The conclusion here is that regularisation does indeed improve the
bfgs
method, and this is through better initial step lengths calculations.

It should be noted that when a Hessian calculation is made, there is no need for a separate gradient calculation (see
chapter

5
). The only gradient calculations needed for the Newton-
grape
method are those during the line search, where no Hessian is calculated. There is redundancy when the Newton-Raphson method reaches quadratic convergence, when the step length is the Newton-step; a gradient calculation must be made at the start of a line search to check that no line search is needed. This accounts for the larger than expected calculation count in
fig.

7

b
. If this redundancy can be removed, with a method that can switch the line search off when it detects quadratic convergence – the gradient count for the Newton-
grape
method with
rfo
would have between 10-20 fewer gradient calculations
9
9

9

There is no such switching method for the
bfgs
method because the Hessian update occurs after the line search, whereas the Newton-Raphson method calculates a Hessian before a line search.
.

in Hz

\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{x}^{(\text{N})}

Figure 11:

Three optimisation methods were compared:

1

Newton-
grape
method using its Newton-step i.e. without a line search. Hessian regularisation is performed with
rfo
, and iterative conditioning bounds the Hessian to
\zeta<1\times 10^{4}
with
\phi=0.9
in
eq.

32
.

2

\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
using a bracketing and sectioning line search, with cubic interpolation during the sectioning phase.

3

grape
method (gradient ascent) using a bracketing and sectioning line search, with cubic interpolation during the sectioning phase.

fig.

11
compares the convergence of the three optimisation methods, plotting
1-\sqrt{2}\cdot\mathcal{J}_{1}
on a log scale to clearly see the convergence to the maximum fidelity value of
\mathcal{J}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.

The Newton-
grape
performs well for this optimisation – without the need for a line search, the method reaches quadratic convergence after a small number of iterations and full quadratic convergence occurs in a small range of iterations,
\penalty\ 15-20
. Admittedly, this is an easy optimisation with only a single spin, and the
\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
method also performs well considering it only uses gradient calculations. Furthermore, the
\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
method rarely makes use of its available line search, with its rank-2 update giving a Newton-like step length at most iterations. The rank-2 update uses a gradient history to give its search direction, and it should be expected that the algorithm gets better as it has a longer history of gradients – after around
20
iterations in
fig.

11

a
.

The benefit of using a Newton-step for the Newton-
grape
method, needing far fewer gradient evaluations, becomes a small benefit considering that the
\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
method rarely uses the available line search. Furthermore, running the same Newton optimisations with any line search method (not shown here) results in the same – the method rarely uses a step length other than
1
. The effect is to double the number of gradient evaluations without any increase in the convergence rate.

There are a few important uses of this test system. The first is that it shows the calculated Hessian is the correct one, removing any doubt that it contains numerical errors. The second is that it is a useful test to show how the condition number inflates compared to the optimisation of the previous section
section

1
.
fig.

8
compares the number of conditioning iterations to those of
section

1
– showing a very different profile of how the condition number increases as the optimisation progresses. The HCF system gradually increases its condition number, starting from the first iteration, but the
14
N system does not need any conditioning until it reaches a certain fidelity, at which point the condition number increases exponentially with the fidelity increase.

§ 3  Universal rotation

This system consists of a
101
non-interacting
\mathrm{spin-{1}}

13
C spins, spread over
50
kHz, in a
14.1
tesla magnetic field. Relaxation is included in the form of the
T_{1}/T_{2}
approximation, keeping only the diagonal terms of the relaxation superoperator. Longitudinal states, corresponding to
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}_{z}
, relax with a rate of
10
Hz, and the transverse states, corresponding to
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{L}}}}_{\pm}
, relax at a rate of
20
Hz.

Figure 12:
Comparison of optimisation methods for a universal rotation in 3D, on a
101
non-interacting
\mathrm{spin-{1}}

13
C spins, spread over
50
kHz. The Hamiltonian includes relaxation with the
T_{1}/T_{2}
approximation. Two methods are compared: Newton-
grape
method using a bracketing and sectioning line search;
\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
using a bracketing and sectioning line search.
a
Comparing the iterations count.
b
Comparing the gradient/Hessian calculation count.

The initial and final states of the system are set as a universal rotation in 3D, mentioned in
section

4
:

\begin{matrix}\big|\rho_{1}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{z}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{x}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{1}\big\rangle\\
\big|\rho_{2}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{x}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{z}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{2}\big\rangle\\
\big|\rho_{3}(0)\big\rangle=+\big|L_{y}\big\rangle&\longrightarrow&-\big|L_{y}\big\rangle=\big|\sigma_{3}\big\rangle\end{matrix}

The total control pulse duration is
5
ms and the sequence is discretised to
400
equal time slices. Control operators are
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{x}^{(\text{C})}
and
\mathchoice{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.14584pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.46875pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}{\hat{\vphantom{\rule{1.0pt}{6.92082pt}}\smash{\hat{\mathcal{H}}}}}_{y}^{(\text{C})}
operators, although the optimisation variables are the pulse sequence phase in
eq.

39
. During the optimisation, the control phase,
\bm{c}_{\varphi}^{\vphantom{|}}
is modified and the control amplitude,
\bm{c}_{A}^{\vphantom{|}}
is kept constant as a shaped amplitude profile:
{}^{1}\!/_{\!2}(1+\sin(\theta))
,
\forall\,\theta\in[0,\pi]
. Pulses are designed to be robust to
\mathrm{B_{1}}
miscalibration by simulating over a range of power levels between
3.75
kHz and
6.25
kHz. This is achieved by simulating 5 optimisations over the given range, then averaging their fidelity, gradient and Hessian to be fed back to the optimisation algorithm.

As can be seen in
fig.

12
, quadratic convergence is not reached. This may be attributed to a solution not existing for this universal rotation, or that the Newton-
grape
method may not perform well when finding robust solutions. Evidence leans to the latter, where the
\ell
-
bfgs
-
grape
reaches a similar convergence after an initial phase of fast convergence. Finding a solution robust over the given range, in addition to finding a universal rotation pulse that works at each power level in the range, seem too much for either algorithm to handle effectively. However, the Newton-
grape
method did show a marginally quicker initial convergence, although the number of gradient/Hessian calculations was similar. The tests on this type of optimisation problem indicate further work is required to find a more effective algorithm.

Propagator Directional Derivatives

“Learning does not make one learned: there are those who have knowledge and those who have understanding. The first requires memory and the second philosophy.”

– Alexandre Dumas,
The Count of Monte Cristo

In a general context, magnetic resonance simulation frequently requires chained exponential integrals of the form

\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\dd t_{1}\!\int\limits_{0}^{t_{1}}\!\dd t_{2}\cdots\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t_{r-2}}\!\!\dd t_{r-1}\Big\{\e^{(t-t_{1})\mathbf{A}_{1}}\mathbf{B}_{1}\e^{(t_{1}-t_{2})\mathbf{A}_{2}}\mathbf{B}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{B}_{r-1}\e^{t_{r}\mathbf{A}_{r}}\Big\}

(1)

where
\mathbf{A}_{r}
and
\mathbf{B}_{r}
are square matrices. Examples include perturbative relaxation theories
[
250
,
164
,
103
]
, reaction yield expressions in radical pair dynamics
[
115
,
306
,
140
]
, average Hamiltonian theory
[
116
]
, and pulsed field gradient propagators in nuclear magnetic resonance
[
67
]
. Their common feature is the complexity of evaluation: expensive matrix factorisations are usually required
[
103
,
1
,
73
]
.

The exponentiation of a matrix
[
172
]
is far from a trivial operation. The operation was recognised as an important tool for solving differential equations
[
88
]
– optimal control methods rely heavily on it. The literature review in
chapter

1
identified the bottleneck in computation during the
grape
method as calculation of a matrix exponential
[
111
,
278
,
311
,
10
,
86
]
. An outline of the
grape
method was presented in
chapter

3
, with the stages of forward/backward propagation
eqs.

76
,
78
,
79
, and calculation of propagator derivatives
eqs.

76
,
80a
,
80b
,
80c
, requiring exponentiation of a matrix. The second-order derivatives of propagators used to construct a Hessian matrix in
chapter

4
also require the exponentiation of a matrix
eqs.

5a
,
5b
,
5c
,
6a
,
6b
,
6c
.

This chapter will introduce the matrix exponential in
section

1
and outline some of the “
nineteen dubious way to compute the exponential of a matrix
’’ to compute this, from the well cited work of Cleve Moler
1
1

1

It is no coincidence that Cleve Moler was the inventor of Matlab and at the forefront of research into numerical computation and matrix computations. He was also the doctoral advisor of Charles Van Loan.
and Charles Van Loan
[
211
,
212
]
. Much of the work used in this chapter can be found in textbooks on computations of matrix functions
[
106
,
127
]
.

The derivatives required for
grape
are directional derivatives of the matrix exponential and these are defined in
sections

1
,
2
. With specific application to optimal control
[
154
,
311
,
86
]
, chained exponential integrals will be used to find to analytic derivatives required by
grape
, with a novel technique presented
section

2
and algorithmic subtleties presented in
section

4
with a measure of how parallel the algorithm is in
section

3
using propagator recycling in
section

5
.

§ 1  The Matrix Exponential

The matrix exponential operation is not a trivial one
[
195
]
and approximations must be used to numerically compute the matrix exponential within Matlab
[
126
,
3
]
. Much of the material in this section is taken from derivations in
[
106
,
127
]
. An indispensable resource, by Moler and van Loan
[
211
,
212
]
outlines an extensive review, showing “
nineteen dubious way to compute the exponential of a matrix
”.

§ 1  Taylor series approximation

The Taylor series
[
302
]
has already been used to form the quadratic model used as the basis of Newton-type methods of numerical optimisation in
eq.

3
. The Taylor series is also the first call in approximating the function of matrix,
f(\mathbf{M})
, and is obtained with the direct application of its scalar counterpart:

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle=f(z_{0})+\frac{\nabla f(z_{0})}{1!}\big(\mathbf{M}-z_{0}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)+\frac{\nabla^{2}f(z_{0})}{2!}\big(\mathbf{M}-z_{0}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)^{2}+\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{\nabla^{r}f(z_{0})}{r!}\big(\mathbf{M}-z_{0}\bm{\mathbbm{1}}\big)^{r}

(2)

where
\nabla^{r}f(z_{0})
is the
r^{\text{th}}
derivative of
f
at
z_{0}
. More specifically, the MacLaurin series
[
201
]
, when
z_{0}=0
above, of the scalar exponential function translates directly to the matrix exponential function of
\e^{t\mathbf{M}}

[
172
]
:

\displaystyle\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}+\frac{t\mathbf{M}}{1!}+\frac{t^{2}\mathbf{M}^{2}}{2!}+\frac{t^{3}\mathbf{M}^{3}}{3!}+\cdots=\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{r}\mathbf{M}^{r}}{r!},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n\times n)}

(3)

In computing the exponential of a matrix, the Taylor series above should be summed to an appropriate number of terms, until adding another term with floating point arithmetic does not alter the numbers stored in the computer. This method is known to give bad results, even in the scalar case, when the series is truncated too soon – when the approximation of what “does not alter the numbers” is a large increment compared to
eps

[
211
]
. The error bound on this truncation is considered in more depth in
[
193
,
206
,
127
]
.

The attraction of the Taylor series approximation, when truncated appropriately, is that it only involves matrix multiplications. Furthermore, the computation of the powers of a matrix
\mathbf{M}^{r}
in
eq.

3
is made more efficient by expressing the matrix power in a binary expansion
[
106
]
e.g.
\mathbf{M}^{13}
requires 5 matrix multiplications:

\mathbf{M}^{13}=\Big(\big(\mathbf{M}^{2}\big)^{2}\Big)^{2}\big(\mathbf{M}^{2}\big)^{2}\mathbf{M}

The
Spinach

[
130
]
implementation of the matrix exponential uses a Taylor series approximation with scaling and squaring (see
section

4
below), designed to preserve sparsity, essential for large matrix dimensions –
nmr
systems are in the millions, common in
nmr

[
66
]
).

Spin Hamiltonians are guaranteed to be sparse in the Pauli basis
[
61
,
316
,
67
]
and their exponential propagators are also sparse when
\|\mathbf{H}\Delta t\|<1
, if care is taken to eliminate insignificant elements after each matrix multiplication in the scaled and squared Taylor series procedure.

§ 2  Padé approximation

A rational function approximation of a matrix is directly translated from that of a scalar function to give

R_{pq}(\mathbf{M})=\big[D_{pq}(\mathbf{M})\big]^{-1}N_{pq}(\mathbf{M})

(4)

where
N_{pq}
and
D_{pq}
are polynomials in
\mathbf{M}
of at most degree-
p
of
N_{pq}
and
q
of
D_{pq}
. The numerator polynomial is

N_{pq}(\mathbf{M})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{p}\frac{(p+q-k)!p!}{(p+q)!k!(p-k)!}\mathbf{M}^{k}

(5)

and the denominator polynomial is

D_{pq}(\mathbf{M})=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{q}\frac{(p+q-k)!q!}{(p+q)!k!(q-k)!}(-\mathbf{M})^{k}

(6)

When
q=0
, the rational function approximation in
eq.

4
is the Taylor series truncated to order-
p
.

If
R_{pq}(\mathbf{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{(p\times q)}
and
D_{pq}(\mathbf{0})=\bm{\mathbbm{1}}
, the rational function approximation in
eq.

4
is the
[p/q]
Padé approximant
[
234
]
. The exact expression for the Padé approximation of the matrix exponential is
[
211
,
212
]

\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=R_{pq}(t\mathbf{M})+\frac{(-1)^{q}}{(p+q)!}(t\mathbf{M})^{p+q+1}\big[D_{pq}(t\mathbf{M})\big]^{-1}\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{1}\!u^{p}(1-u)^{q}\e^{t\mathbf{M}(1-u)}\dd u

(7)

Diagonal approximants, where
p=q
, are more accurate than when
p\neq q
and are preferred in practice because
R_{rr}(\mathbf{M})
, with
r=\max(p,q)
, has the same computational cost as when
p\neq q

[
127
]
.

As it stands in
eq.

7
, the Padé approximant is only a good approximation of the matrix exponential near the origin, when
\|\mathbf{M}\|
is small
[
211
,
212
,
106
]
(although the Chebyshev rational approximation can perform well in some circumstance
section

3
) – requiring the
scaling and squaring
technique in
section

4
to overcome the problem.

It should be noted that calculation of the “perilous inverse” matrix
\big[D_{pq}(\mathbf{M})\big]^{-1}
is required, which can be an undesirable calculation
[
196
]
.

§ 3  Chebyshev rational approximation

If
\mathbf{M}
is a Hermitian matrix, then approximating its exponential amounts to approximating the exponential function on the spectrum of eigenvalues of
\mathbf{M}

[
127
]
. The Chebyshev rational approximation
2
2

2

The Chebyshev rational approximation is also known at the
Best L
∞
approximation
, or the
minimax
approximation
[
127
]
.
, like the Padé approximant of
section

2
above, is based on the rational function approximation of
eq.

4
.

C_{qq}(\mathbf{M})=\big[D_{qq}(\mathbf{M})\big]^{-1}N_{qq}(\mathbf{M})

(8)

where only the diagonal approximants are considered. The polynomials,
D_{qq}(\mathbf{M})
and
N_{qq}(\mathbf{M})
, of order-
q
are those defined in
eqs.

5
,
6
. A minimiser for
\|C_{qq}(\mathbf{M})-\e^{\mathbf{M}}\|
can be found from coefficients determined in
[
42
,
38
]
, where
\mathbf{M}
is Hermitian with negative eigenvalues. This is a useful method for sparse matrices. Unlike the Padé approximation of the matrix exponential, which is only valid near the origin, the Chebyshev rational approximation is valid for the whole negative real axis (or the whole positive real axis for
\e^{-\mathbf{M}}
).

A success of this approximation is, when considering
\e^{\mathbf{M}\Delta t}
with a time step
\Delta t
, that the Chebyshev approximation allows much larger time steps compared to the Padé approximant
[
91
]
. However, Chebyshev series diverge with non-Hermitian matrices
[
219
]
, where the error bounds for the scalar problem do not translate to matrices
[
127
]
– the method is expected to fail when considering dissipative dynamics.

§ 4  Scaling and squaring

The scaling and squaring technique
[
180
,
325
]
exploits the relationship

\displaystyle\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=\Big(\e^{\frac{t\mathbf{M}}{s}}\Big)^{s},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n\times n)}

(9)

This can be used to control the round-off errors associated with the Taylor and Padé approximants: as
t\|\mathbf{M}\|
or the spread of eigenvalues increases
[
211
,
212
]
. The choice of
s
to be the smallest power of
2
for which
{}^{\|\mathbf{M}\|}\!/_{\!s}\leqslant 1

[
211
,
196
]
:

\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=\Big(\e^{\frac{t\mathbf{M}}{2^{m}}}\Big)^{2^{m}}

(10)

Further efficiencies and error tolerance have been developed, the Padé approximants with scaling and squaring is the method of matrix exponential used in both Matlab and Mathematica
[
126
,
3
]
. An attraction of its use with the Taylor series is that only approximate scaling is required
[
219
,
132
]
.

The next section
section

2
will use a block triangular form
[
235
]
of the matrix
\mathbf{M}
, for the calculation of directional derivatives:

\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}

(11)

The method of Padé approximants with scaling and squaring shows weakness when exponentiating block triangular matrices when
\|\mathbf{B}\|\gg\max\big(\|\mathbf{A}\|,\|\mathbf{D}\|\big)

[
127
]
: the result is that the diagonal blocks are over-scaled
[
149
,
54
]
.

§ 2  Directional derivatives

Derivatives of functions of matrices are called
directional derivatives
, or
Gâteaux derivatives
. which are defined in
[
127
]
as

D_{\mathbf{B}}(f(\mathbf{A}))\triangleq\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{f(\mathbf{A}+h\mathbf{B})-f(\mathbf{A})}{h}=\frac{\dd}{\dd h}\bigg|_{h=0}\!\!\!f(\mathbf{A}+h\mathbf{B})

(12)

This has a similar form to the finite difference equation in
eq.

12a
and the Taylor series truncated to first order in
eq.

3
, and should be considered the formal definition of the derivative of the function of a matrix
3
3

3

A directional derivative has a stronger notion of differentiability when it is a
Frechét derivative
. This is true if
\exists D_{\mathbf{B}}(f(\mathbf{A}))
is a linear function of
\mathbf{B}
and continuous in
\mathbf{A}

[
127
]
. Assuming these qualifications are met, which is the case in this thesis, the terms directional derivative and Frechét derivative are equivalent. The remainder of this thesis will use the more descriptive term
directional derivative
.
. The interpretation of
D_{\mathbf{B}}(f(\mathbf{A}))
is:
the derivative of the function
f
at
\mathbf{A}
in the direction
\mathbf{B}

[
218
,
127
]
.

§ 1  Directional derivatives of the matrix exponential

The directional derivative of the matrix exponential,
\e^{t\mathbf{A}}
, is then

D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\e^{t(\mathbf{A}+h\mathbf{B})}-\e^{t\mathbf{A}}}{h}=\frac{\dd}{\dd h}\bigg|_{h=0}\!\!\!\e^{t(\mathbf{A}+h\mathbf{B})}

(13)

which can also be expanded as a Taylor series
[
127
]
:

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle=\e^{t(\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B})}-\e^{t\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{1!}\mathbf{B}+\frac{t^{2}}{2!}\big(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}\!\big)+\frac{t^{3}}{3!}\big(\mathbf{A}^{\!2}\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{\!2}\!\big)+\cdots

(14)

This relates to the power directional derivatives
[
218
]
by

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{r}}{r!}\frac{\dd^{\,r}}{\dd t^{r}}D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})\bigg|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{r}}{r!}D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{\mathbf{A}^{r}})

(15)

which leads to the
Magnus expansions

[
203
,
116
]
.

An alternative, and more useful representation than
eqs.

13
,
14

[
218
]
is the integral representation
[
144
,
74
]
:

D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})=\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\e^{(t-s)\mathbf{A}\!}\mathbf{B}\e^{s\mathbf{A}}\dd s

(16)

This will become a useful representation in the following sections. It is useful to note that rearranging this formula
[
218
]
leads to a commutator series
[
120
,
203
]

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\e^{s\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{B}\e^{-s\mathbf{A}}\dd s\Bigg]\e^{t\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\mathbf{B},\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\e^{s\mathbf{A}}\dd s\bigg\}\e^{t\mathbf{A}}=\bigg\{\mathbf{B},\frac{\e^{t\mathbf{A}}-\bm{\mathbbm{1}}}{\mathbf{A}}\bigg\}\e^{t\mathbf{A}}

\displaystyle=\Bigg[\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{r+1}}{(r+1)!}\big\{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}^{\!r}\big\}\Bigg]\e^{t\mathbf{A}}

(17)

where the notation for the commutator power series relates to the commutator by
\big\{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}^{\!r}\big\}=\Big[\big\{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}^{\!r-1}\big\},\mathbf{A}\Big]
.

§ 2  Block triangular auxiliary matrix exponential

A method for computing these chained exponential integrals was proposed by Van Loan in 1978
[
196
]
. While investigating the sensitivity of the matrix exponential
[
195
]
, he noted that the integrals in question are solutions to linear block matrix differential equations and suggested that block matrix exponentials are used to compute them. In the simplest case of a single integral
[
143
]
, a block triangular auxiliary matrix
[
235
]
is exponentiated:

\displaystyle\e^{\mathbf{M}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\e^{\mathbf{A}}&\int\limits_{0}^{1}\!\e^{(1-s)\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{B}\e^{s\mathbf{A}}\dd s\\
\mathbf{0}&\e^{\mathbf{A}}\end{pmatrix}

(18a)

\displaystyle\e^{t\mathbf{M}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\e^{t\mathbf{A}}&\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\e^{(t-s)\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{B}\e^{s\mathbf{A}}\dd s\\
\mathbf{0}&\e^{t\mathbf{A}}\end{pmatrix}

(18b)

where the above-diagonal block is easily identified as directional derivative in the integral representation of
eq.

16

[
144
,
74
]
. Now
eq.

18b
can be recast in a form to show explicitly the first directional derivative of the matrix exponential:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{A}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=\begin{pmatrix}\e^{t\mathbf{A}}&D_{\mathbf{B}}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})\\
\mathbf{0}&\e^{t\mathbf{A}}\end{pmatrix}

(19)

Further to
eq.

16
, the
r^{\text{th}}
directional derivative can be defined recursively
[
218
]
by

D_{\mathbf{B}}^{r}(\e^{t\mathbf{A}})=r\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\e^{\mathbf{A}(t-s)}\mathbf{B}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{r-1}(\e^{s\mathbf{A}})\dd s

(20)

It is useful to see the pattern when using larger block-bidiagonal, semi-circulant auxiliary matrices. Higher order directional derivatives of the matrix exponential can be calculated by constructing and exponentiating sparse block-bidiagonal semi-circulant matrices
[
218
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{B}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{A}&\ddots&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&\mathbf{B}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{A}\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\e^{t\mathbf{M}}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{0!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{0}&\frac{1}{1!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}\;&\frac{1}{2!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{2}\;&\cdots&\frac{1}{r!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{r}\;\\
\mathbf{0}&\frac{1}{0!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{0}&\frac{1}{1!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}\;&\cdots&\frac{1}{(r-1)!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{r-1}\;\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\frac{1}{0!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{0}&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&\frac{1}{1!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}\;\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\frac{1}{0!}D_{\mathbf{B}}^{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(21)

This is set out in a general way in
[
218
]
, and for this specific case of the first directional derivative in
[
81
]
; derivation of the latter and for that of the second directional derivative is set out in the next section
section

3
.

Van Loan’s method was subsequently refined by Carbonell et. al.
[
37
]
, deriving a convenient expression using the exponential of a block-bidiagonal semi-circulant auxiliary matrix:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}{{\mathbf{A}}_{1}}&{{\mathbf{B}}_{1}}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{A}}_{2}}&{{\mathbf{B}}_{2}}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{A}}_{3}}&\ddots&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&{{\mathbf{B}}_{r-1}}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{A}}_{r}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\e^{\mathbf{M}\!t}=\begin{pmatrix}{{\mathbf{D}}_{11}}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{12}}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{13}}&\cdots&{{\mathbf{D}}_{1r}}\\
\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{22}}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{23}}&\cdots&{{\mathbf{D}}_{2r}}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{33}}&\ddots&\vdots\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\ddots&{{\mathbf{D}}_{r-1,r}}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&{{\mathbf{D}}_{rr}}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(22)

The integrals in question populate the rows of the resulting block matrix, having the chained integral form of
eq.

1
, e.g.

\mathbf{D}_{1r}=\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t}\!\dd t_{1}\!\int\limits_{0}^{t_{1}}\!\dd t_{2}\cdots\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{t_{r-2}}\!\!\dd t_{r-1}\Big\{\e^{(t-t_{1})\mathbf{A}_{1}}\mathbf{B}_{1}\e^{(t_{1}-t_{2})\mathbf{A}_{2}}\mathbf{B}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{B}_{r-1}\e^{t_{r}\mathbf{A}_{r}}\Big\}

(23)

§ 3  Directional derivativesof the Propagator

Fidelity functional derivatives of optimal control
[
311
,
86
]
are a form of chained exponential integrals which have expensive matrix factorization in their evaluation
[
103
,
73
,
1
]
. A solution to this problem is the observation that matrix exponentiation does not require factorisations and preserves the spin operator sparsity
[
166
,
130
]
and the use of an auxiliary matrix technique
[
196
,
218
,
37
,
81
]
.

The
grape
method was shown in
chapters

3
,
4
to discretise time and solve
eq.

61
using thin slice propagators. From the computational efficiency point of view, the central problem is therefore the calculation of first derivatives of the fidelity functional in
eqs.

80a
,
80b
,
80c
and mixed second derivatives in
eqs.

7a
,
7b
,
7c
, e.g.

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{J}_{0}}{\partial\,{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}=\Big\langle\chi_{n+1}\Big|D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\big)\Big|\rho_{n-1}\Big\rangle

(24a)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}_{0}}{\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,m}^{\vphantom{|}}}=\Big\langle\chi_{n+1}\Big|D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\big)\mathbf{P}\!_{n-1}\cdots\mathbf{P}\!_{m+1}D_{\mathbf{H}_{j}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{m}\big)\Big|\rho_{m-1}\Big\rangle

(24b)

where time slice propagators (assuming a fixed time grid step
\Delta t
) are defined in
eq.

76
as

\mathbf{P}\!_{n}=\exp{\Big[\!-i\mathbf{H}_{n}\Delta t\Big]}=\exp{\bigg[\!-i\Big(\mathbf{H}_{0}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\Big)\Delta t\bigg]}

(25)

where the relaxation term is removed and the double-hat superoperator notation is replaced by a matrix representation bold-font for notational brevity.

In addition to the first derivative of the propagators, the second derivatives are required for the construction of the Hessian block diagonal. After separating the cases where efficiencies can be made with communing control operators, discussed in
section

3
, the remaining calculations from
eqs.

5a
,
5b
,
5c
,
6a
,
6b
,
6c
are

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}_{0}}{\partial{{c}}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\,2}}=\Big\langle\chi_{n+1}\Big|D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\Big|\rho_{n-1}\Big\rangle

(26a)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{\,2}\!\mathcal{J}_{0}}{\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}=\Big\langle\chi_{n+1}\Big|D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}\mathbf{H}_{j}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\Big|\rho_{n-1}\Big\rangle

(26b)

The primary task therefore is to calculate first and second directional derivatives of the slice propagators.

§ 1  First directional derivatives

The original derivation of the first propagator directional derivative from
[
154
]
follows by considering a small perturbation to the control amplitude
\delta{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}
on the control operator to derive the change in the propagator,
\delta\mathbf{P}\!_{n}
, then to form a derivative from
\delta\mathbf{P}\!_{n}/\delta{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}
. Using
eqs.

12
,
25
this can be written as

\frac{\delta\mathbf{P}\!_{n}}{\delta{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle n}^{\vphantom{|}}}=D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)=\Bigg(\lim_{\big\{{\displaystyle{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}\!{\scriptstyle\to\,0}\big\}}\!\!\frac{\exp\big[-i\mathbf{H}_{n}\Delta t\big]-\exp\big[-i\mathbf{H}_{0}\Delta t\big]}{{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}\Bigg)

(27)

The procedure in
[
154
]
then finds solution by truncating
eq.

17
to first order in
\Delta t
, giving

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)=\Delta t\Big[\mathbf{H}_{k},\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\Big],

\displaystyle\Delta t\ll\Big\|\mathbf{H}_{0}+\sum\limits_{k=1}^{K}{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\Big\|^{-1}

(28)

An alternative representation of
eq.

27
is the integral form of
eq.

16
:

D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)=\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{-i\Delta t}\!\e^{(-i\Delta t-\tau)\mathbf{H}_{n}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\e^{\tau\mathbf{H}_{n}}\dd\tau

(29)

Using
eq.

18b
and setting
\mathbf{A}\leftarrow\mathbf{H}_{n}
,
\mathbf{B}\leftarrow\mathbf{H}_{k}
, and
t\leftarrow-i\Delta t
, gives the block triangular auxiliary matrix exponential as
[
81
]

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{k}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\e^{-i\mathbf{L}_{2}\Delta t}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}\!_{n}&D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\end{pmatrix}

(30)

where the above-diagonal is the first directional derivative required for gradient element calculation in
eqs.

24a
,
24b
.

§ 2  Second directional derivatives

The integral representation of the second directional derivatives form a chained integral as in
eq.

1
:

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)=\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{-i\Delta t}\int\limits_{0}^{\tau}\e^{(-i\Delta t-\tau)\mathbf{H}_{n}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\e^{(\tau-\upsilon)\mathbf{H}_{n}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\e^{\upsilon\mathbf{H}_{n}}\dd\upsilon\dd\tau

(31a)

\displaystyle D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}\mathbf{H}_{j}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)=\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{0}^{-i\Delta t}\int\limits_{0}^{\tau}\e^{(-i\Delta t-\tau)\mathbf{H}_{n}}\mathbf{H}_{k}\e^{(\tau-\upsilon)\mathbf{H}_{n}}\mathbf{H}_{j}\e^{\upsilon\mathbf{H}_{n}}\dd\upsilon\dd\tau

(31b)

Just as in the previous calculation of first directional derivatives in
eq.

30
,
eq.

21
shows a similar auxiliary matrix technique can be used to calculate higher order directional derivatives. Calculation of the second directional derivatives requires a
3\times 3
bidiagonal block matrix auxiliary matrix from
eq.

21
,

\mathbf{L}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{j}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}

(32)

where the upper-diagonal blocks contain the two control operators needed for the mixed second directional derivatives. Following from
eqs.

22
,
23
, the exponential of the auxiliary matrix
\mathbf{L}_{3}
is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}\!_{n}&D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)&\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{P}\!_{n}&D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\end{pmatrix}\!,

\displaystyle j=k

(33a)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{P}\!_{n}&D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)&\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathbf{H}_{k}\mathbf{H}_{j}}^{2}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{P}\!_{n}&D_{\mathbf{H}_{j}}\!\big(\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\!\big)\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{P}\!_{n}\end{pmatrix}\!,

\displaystyle j\neq k

(33b)

where the upper-right blocks are the second directional derivatives required for Hessian element calculation in
eqs.

26a
,
26b
.

§ 4  Krylov subspace techniques

There is an important efficiency to be made when considering the propagator derivatives of in that only the resulting vector
\big|\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{n+1}}{\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}\big\rangle
is required in
eqs.

24a
,
26a
,
26b
. The solutions to these equations are of the form
f(\mathbf{M})b
– the action of
f(\mathbf{M})
on the vector
b
is required
[
321
]
. This type of problem is readily solved with
Krylov subspace methods
, such as the
Arnoldi process

[
9
]
or the
Lanczos process

[
173
]
, avoiding explicit calculation of the matrix exponential
[
256
,
128
,
127
,
106
]
.

The Arnoldi process is implemented in
Spinach
to efficiently calculate
[
170
,
165
]
the exponential of a Liouvillian multiplied by a state vector. The effect is that forward and backward propagation can be calculated in this way. Furthermore, the effect of first and second derivatives on the forward propagated state vector can be calculated with their auxiliary matrices:

Forward propagation:

\displaystyle\Big[\exp\begin{pmatrix}-i\Delta t\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\!\Big]\,\Big|\rho_{n}\Big\rangle=\Big|\rho_{n+1}\Big\rangle

(34a)

Backward propagation:

\displaystyle\Big[\exp\begin{pmatrix}+i\Delta t\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}\!\Big]\,\Big|\chi_{n}\Big\rangle=\Big|\chi_{n-1}\Big\rangle

(34b)

Propagated first directional derivative:

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(\!\!-i\Delta t\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{k}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}_{\vphantom{|}}^{\vphantom{|}}\!\!\right)\!\right]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\Big|\rho_{n}\Big\rangle\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Big|\frac{\partial\rho_{n+1}}{\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}\Big\rangle\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}

(34c)

Propagated second directional derivative:

\displaystyle\left[\exp\left(\!-i\Delta t\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{k}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}&\mathbf{H}_{j}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{H}_{n}\end{pmatrix}_{\vphantom{|}}^{\vphantom{|}}\!\right)\right]\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}\\
\Big|\rho_{n}\Big\rangle\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\Big|\frac{\partial^{2}\rho_{n+1}}{\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle k,n}^{\vphantom{|}}\partial{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle j,n}^{\vphantom{|}}}\Big\rangle\\
\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{bmatrix}

(34d)

§ 5  Propagator recycling

Two computational efficiencies should be taken into account when calculating the full Hessian matrix of
fig.

1
. Firstly, the first propagator direction derivatives do not need to be calculated with
eq.

30
when a Hessian calculation is made: these directional derivatives are already contained in the above-diagonal of
eq.

33b
. Secondly, Krylov subspace techniques can be used to calculate the effect of the propagator directional derivative on the state
\big|\rho_{n}\big\rangle
in some of
eqs.

24a
,
24b
,
26a
,
26b
. The following lists outline the type of calculation recommended to construct fidelity derivatives. There are two cases:

1

A fidelity and gradient vector calculation for a gradient ascent method, quasi-Newton method, or a line search method.

2

The simultaneous calculation of the fidelity, gradient vector and Hessian matrix for a Newton step calculation.

§ 1  Fidelity and gradient calculation

=2\times K\times N
.

.
